Cooperation thrives when moral judgment matches
moral conduct
Jorge Peña1,∗, Julian Garcia2, and Toby Handfield3
1Department of Social and Behavioral Sciences, Toulouse School of Economics, Toulouse, France
2Department of Data Science and AI, Faculty of Information Technology, Monash University, Melbourne,
Australia
3School of Philosophical, Historical and International Studies, Monash University, Melbourne, Australia
∗Corresponding author: jorge.pena@tse-fr.eu
A leading explanation for why humans cooperate with strangers is indirect reciprocity: when
conduct is observed, individuals earn good reputations by cooperating with the deserving1,2.
Previous research has identified many ‘moral systems’ that sustain cooperation through
indirect reciprocity – each pairing a strategy for how to act with a norm for how to judge
others – yet what makes them succeed, and whether that success generalizes beyond the
donation game, has remained unclear3–5. Here we evaluate all 1,048,576 moral systems
whose norms condition on both players’ reputations and actions, across every two-player
social dilemma. We find that every highly stable, cooperative system pairs a ‘discriminating’
strategy – one that cooperates with the good and defects against the bad when in good
standing – with a ‘consistent’ norm that deems good exactly those who act as the strategy
prescribes. Such systems ‘practice what they preach’ and preach what they practice. In the
limit of small errors, only one strategy paired with a consistent norm remains stable across
all social dilemmas: the ‘contrite discriminator’, which cooperates to make amends when
its standing is bad. Our results unify decades of research on indirect reciprocity, revealing
moral consistency as the common ingredient of every stable, cooperative moral system.
Humans cooperate on a scale unmatched among other animals, routinely helping people they
may never encounter again6,7. A central explanation is reciprocity. Under direct reciprocity,
individuals help those who have helped them8; under indirect reciprocity, they help those who
have helped others9–11, so that cooperation is rewarded not by second parties but by third parties
who have observed or heard about one’s conduct1. A reputation aggregates an individual’s past
conduct into a standing that others can act on without any direct experience of them4. What turns
such standings into cooperation is the ‘moral system’ adopted by society at large: the rules of
1
arXiv:2610.03832v1  [physics.soc-ph]  2 Oct 2026


behavior and approval about “what is right and wrong”1, typically formalized as a strategy paired
with a social norm.
Prior work has uncovered an array of moral systems capable of sustaining cooperation through
indirect reciprocity, the best known being the ‘leading eight’ first identified by Ohtsuki and Iwasa3
and studied extensively since12–16. Yet this body of work has three important limitations. First,
most of what we know about successful moral systems comes from a single, highly simplified
interaction: the donation game, in which one player decides whether to pay a personal cost to
confer a larger benefit on the other4. This is also the setting for almost all recent theoretical work,
including studies of higher-order norms17, the evolution of norms18,19, and indirect reciprocity
under private reputations20–23. Real-world human cooperation, by contrast, unfolds across a far
wider range of situations. These certainly include prisoner’s dilemmas – of which the donation
game is a special case – where cooperation is dominated. But they also include snowdrift (or
chicken) games, where the best response to defection is to cooperate, and stag hunts (or assurance
games), where each player is best off matching the other’s action24–26. Second, previous analyses
have each searched only a small subset of the possible social norms, ignoring some of the
information available to observers in a single interaction3,5. Third, and most fundamentally, we
lack a unifying account of why some moral systems succeed. Existing characterizations are
disparate and tied to particular settings, leaving no parsimonious explanation for what separates
the moral systems that sustain cooperation from those that fail. Even the study that covered the
largest range of games concluded that its successful norms “have different principles in different
dilemmas for suppressing cheaters”5.
Here we address all three limitations at once. As in most models of indirect reciprocity3,4, we
take actions and reputations to be binary: an individual either cooperates or defects, and is
judged either good or bad. We study the complete space of social norms that condition on the
two players’ reputations and actions. Combined with the 24 = 16 possible strategies, this yields
220 = 1,048,576 moral systems, which we evaluate across the entire set of symmetric two-player
cooperative dilemmas where mutual cooperation is socially optimal24. Our analysis reveals
a single organizing principle behind every successful moral system: whatever the dilemma,
those systems that best sustain cooperation pair a ‘discriminating’ strategy, which conditions
cooperation on the partner’s reputation, with a ‘consistent’ social norm, which awards a good
reputation to exactly those who act as the strategy dictates. Among these consistent systems,
those built on a ‘contrite’ strategy – one that cooperates to make amends when its own standing
is bad – stand out as the only moral systems that remain both stable and cooperative across every
2


class of dilemma at once.
Results
Mapping the full space of moral systems
Modeling indirect reciprocity requires three ingredients (Methods): a payoff matrix, which defines
the rewards and costs of cooperating and defecting (Fig. 1a); a strategy (or action rule), which
specifies when to cooperate and when to defect (Fig. 1b); and a social norm (or assessment
rule), which tells observers when to assign a good or a bad reputation (Fig. 1c). Strategies and
social norms can be classified by the amount and nature of the information they take as input.
For instance, early models considered a moral system where the strategy (‘discriminator’ or
DISC; Fig. 1b) prescribes cooperation if the co-player’s reputation is good and defection if the
co-player’s reputation is bad, and the social norm (‘Scoring’; Fig. 1c) assigns a good reputation
if the player cooperated and a bad reputation if the player defected2. Both the strategy and the
social norm of this moral system require one bit of information, and are thus said to be of first
order. Although this moral system can sustain cooperation against unconditional defectors, it is
not robust to invasion by other strategies27.
Ohtsuki and Iwasa3 were the first to systematically study all moral systems obtained by pairing
each of the 24 = 16 possible strategies of up to second order (based on two bits of information,
namely the reputations of the focal player and the co-player) with each of the 28 possible social
norms of up to third order (based on three bits of information, namely the action of the player
being assessed and the reputations of player and co-player). Out of these 212 = 4,096 moral
systems, only eight – known as the ‘leading eight’ – were found both to sustain high levels of
cooperation and to be evolutionarily stable when social interactions were modeled as a donation
game. Two examples of the leading eight are L6 and L1 (labels adopted from20). L6 employs
DISC together with a second-order social norm (‘Stern Judging’; Fig. 1c) that calls an individual
good only if they cooperated with a good partner or defected against a bad one. In contrast, L1
uses a second-order strategy that takes the individual’s own reputation into account, prescribing
cooperation when the co-player is good or when one is oneself bad. This prescription to cooperate
when in bad standing can be read as a form of contrition that attempts to repair one’s damaged
reputation12; we therefore call this strategy ‘contrite discriminator’ (CDISC; Fig. 1b). L1 pairs
CDISC with the third-order social norm ‘Standing’ (Fig. 1c), which also conditions on the
reputation of the individual being judged: good individuals keep their good reputation unless

whatever the partner’s reputation.
Within indirect reciprocity, social dilemmas beyond the donation game have rarely been studied.
A notable exception is the study by Nakamura and Ohtsuki5, who analyzed indirect reciprocity
across prisoner’s dilemmas, snowdrift games, and stag hunts, and identified which social norms
stabilize cooperation in each game. Building on their work, we ask whether a single moral system
can sustain cooperation across the entire range of cooperative dilemmas.
Prior analyses differ in how much of the information available in a single interaction their social
norms use (Extended Data Fig. 1). The norms studied by Ohtsuki and Iwasa3,12 condition on
the focal player’s action and on both players’ reputations, but never on the co-player’s action;
the norms of Nakamura and Ohtsuki5 condition on both players’ actions and the co-player’s
4


reputation, but never on the focal player’s own reputation. Norms that use all four pieces of
information available in a single interaction – the two players’ reputations and actions – have so far
been left unexplored5. We study the complete fourth-order space of 216 = 65,536 social norms,
which, paired with the 16 strategies of up to second order, yields the 220 = 1,048,576 moral
systems we evaluate across the entire set of symmetric 2 × 2 cooperative dilemmas where mutual
cooperation is socially optimal (Extended Data Fig. 2). Throughout, we adopt a public-assessment
model of reputation: a single observer witnesses each interaction and judges both players, with
the reputations they assign shared by the whole community, so that everyone comes to hold the
same view of any given individual3,28.
Highly stable systems are consistent
We characterize each moral system by two quantities: its cooperation index and its stability index
(see Methods). The cooperation index (𝜅) is the probability that a randomly chosen resident
cooperates at equilibrium. Because this probability does not depend on the payoffs, it is the same
for every game we study. The stability index for a given dilemma class is the fraction of payoff
space over which the resident strategy resists invasion by any alternative strategy, holding fixed
the social norm. We then take the simple average across the three dilemma classes to obtain the
average stability index (𝜎). We report results for low error rates in the execution of cooperation
(𝜖= 0.02) and in the assignment of reputations (𝜒= 0.02), but our conclusions are robust to
other small error rates and to alternative constructions of the stability index (SI Figs. 5, 26, 27).
Computing the cooperation index and the average stability index for all 220 moral systems –
where systems that differ only by swapping the two reputation labels, and thus prescribe identical
behavior, are evaluated only once (SI Section 4.1) – reveals our main result (Fig. 2): a single
cluster of 210 = 1,024 moral systems stands out as being both highly stable and highly cooperative,
separated from the rest by a clear gap in stability. The moral systems in this cluster share two
properties. First, they each use a ‘discriminating’ strategy: players in good standing cooperate with
good co-players and defect against bad ones (the four discriminating strategies – DISC, CDISC,
ODISC, and PDISC – are shown in Fig. 1b; Methods). Second, they pair such a discriminating
strategy with a ‘consistent’ social norm: one that, conditional on the co-player playing the same
strategy, assigns a good reputation to all and only those who behave as the system’s own strategy
prescribes (Fig. 3a; Methods). Because consistency says nothing about cases where the co-player
deviates from the system’s strategy, it leaves eight bits unconstrained, so each strategy admits
28 = 256 consistent norms (for CDISC, these include, for instance, Consistent Standing and
5

moral systems that are distinct up to the relabeling of reputations (one representative per mirror

average stability index (𝜎, vertical axis); here 𝜒= 𝜖= 0.02. The background hexbin layer shows
the density of the 523,776 representatives that are not consistent discriminating systems, on a
logarithmic color scale (inset). The 1,024 consistent discriminating systems (red), shown as a
jittered scatter plot overlaid on the density map, cluster tightly in the upper-right corner, separated

Generous Standing; Fig. 1c). The four discriminating strategies thus give rise to the 1,024 systems
of the cluster, which we call ‘consistent discriminating’. Intuitively, consistency can be likened to
moral integrity and an absence of hypocrisy: the standard to which one holds others is exactly
the standard by which one acts. Interestingly, all leading-eight moral systems use discriminating
strategies (DISC and CDISC), but only two are consistent: L2 and L6 (Fig. 3b).
The success of consistent discriminating systems follows from the basic function of a moral
system in indirect reciprocity: namely, to sort the population so that reciprocators can direct
cooperation toward individuals with the same strategy, and defect against all others. Indeed, a
consistent social norm is the one best suited to this task, as it assigns reputations so that those

Figure 3: Social norms of the consistent discriminating systems and of the leading eight.
a, The social norm classes consistent with each of the four discriminating strategies (Fig. 1b);
because consistency constrains a norm only when the co-player behaves in accordance with the
resident strategy, eight of the sixteen bits are left unconstrained (∗), so each strategy admits
28 = 256 consistent norms. b, The social norms of the leading-eight moral systems. Rows are
labeled by the reputations and action (𝑟1𝑟2𝑎1) of the judged player, with ∗standing for the
co-player’s action 𝑎2, on which these third-order norms do not depend; the shared pattern column

who follow the resident strategy are labeled ‘good’, and those who deviate are labeled ‘bad’. An
inconsistent norm, by contrast, will assign good reputations to some mutant strategies at least as
often as to residents, blurring the very distinction on which discrimination depends, thus opening
the door to invasion. We can make this intuition precise in the absence of execution errors: a
moral system is consistent if and only if, at equilibrium, residents are judged good strictly more
often than any mutant that deviates from the resident strategy (SI Propositions 8, 9).
Contrition makes stability universal
Within the cluster of consistent discriminating systems, a further subdivision emerges according
to whether the strategy is contrite (Fig. 2b). A ‘contrite’ strategy prescribes cooperation whenever
one’s own standing is bad (Methods); among the discriminating strategies, CDISC is the only one
with this property. The 256 consistent systems associated with this contrite discriminator achieve
the highest stability of any moral system for all but the largest assignment error rate we examine
(Fig. 2b; SI Fig. 6), remaining highly stable in every class of dilemma (SI Section 4.4). We
7


further examine this finding analytically in a tractable regime: no execution errors (𝜖= 0) and the
limit of vanishing assignment errors (𝜒→0). Of the 220 moral systems we investigate, 114,688
are ‘righteous’: those in which residents both cooperate at near-maximal rates and almost all
attain good standing (Methods; SI Definition 11, Proposition 5). In structural terms, a righteous
strategy is reputation-sensitive and prescribes cooperation with a good partner when in good
standing. A righteous norm, assessing a resident whose partner also follows the strategy, judges
that resident good in at least three of the four combinations of the two players’ standings – always
when both are good, and in at least two of the remaining three (SI Eq. (80)). The 1,024 consistent
discriminating systems and all leading-eight systems are righteous (SI Propositions 7, 10). We
ask which righteous systems are ‘universally stable’, that is, resistant to invasion across every
cooperative dilemma we consider. We show that a righteous moral system is universally stable if
and only if its strategy is CDISC and its social norm is consistent (SI Theorem 1).
We identify the universally stable systems by discarding, in turn, the righteous systems invadable
by three important classes of strategies (SI Section 3.5; SI Fig. 4). First, resistance to unconditional
defectors (ALLD) requires two conditions. The strategy must prescribe defection against a bad
partner when in good standing; otherwise residents behave indistinguishably from unconditional
cooperators and ALLD invades directly in prisoner’s dilemmas and snowdrift games. All
discriminating strategies meet this condition. The social norm must also judge (mutual) defection
against a good partner, when in bad standing, as bad (SI Propositions 12, 11). Among the consistent
discriminating systems, those with DISC and CDISC as strategies satisfy this condition, whereas
those with ODISC and PDISC do not (SI Proposition 14). All leading-eight systems satisfy this
condition as well (SI Proposition 13). Overall, 12,800 righteous systems resist unconditional
defectors across every dilemma.
Second, resistance to unconditional cooperators (ALLC) is the converse problem. Since a
discriminator in good standing defects only against bad partners, and almost all residents hold
good reputations, an ALLC mutant differs from a resident mainly by cooperating with the rare
bad ones. The social norm must therefore treat this unwarranted cooperation as a moral failure
(SI Propositions 16, 17, 18). The consistent systems built on DISC and CDISC satisfy this
condition, but only four of the leading eight – L2, L5, L6, and L8 – do; L2 and L6 are among
those consistent systems, while L5 and L8 are not. The other four leading-eight systems (L1, L3,
L4, and L7) praise cooperation with a bad partner and are invaded by ALLC in stag hunts (SI
Propositions 20, 19; Fig. 4b). Overall, only 4,608 righteous systems resist both unconditional
defectors and unconditional cooperators across every dilemma.
8


Finally, resistance to mutant discriminating strategies singles out CDISC under a consistent
norm – that is, the consistent contrite discriminating systems. The crucial asymmetry is that
CDISC can invade DISC under a consistent norm, but not vice versa. The two strategies act
differently only when both players are bad: DISC defects, whereas CDISC shows contrition and
cooperates. A consistent DISC norm judges this contrition bad, so a CDISC mutant needs two
rounds to regain good standing, collecting 2𝑆where a DISC resident collects 𝑃+ 𝑅(Extended
Data Fig. 3); whenever 2𝑆> 𝑅+ 𝑃, as is possible in snowdrift games, CDISC invades. Among
CDISC residents under a consistent norm, a DISC mutant defecting in the rare state where both
players are bad earns 𝑇but is judged bad, and collects 𝑇+ 𝑆over the two rounds it takes to regain
good standing; a resident in the same state cooperates, is immediately judged good, and collects
2𝑅(Extended Data Fig. 4). Because mutual cooperation is efficient (2𝑅> 𝑇+ 𝑆), the mutant has
no margin to invade.
CDISC under a consistent norm resists not only rival discriminators but also every alternative
mutant strategy (SI Theorem 1). This is our central analytical result: with no execution errors
and in the limit of vanishing assignment errors, a righteous moral system is evolutionarily stable
across all cooperative dilemmas where mutual cooperation is efficient if and only if its strategy is
discriminating and contrite, and its social norm is consistent with it – the 256 consistent contrite
discriminating systems. Only one member of the leading eight belongs to this set: L2 (CDISC,
Consistent Standing). In this sense, we can call L2 the ‘leading one’. L2 is also the simplest
consistent contrite discriminating system, as its third-order social norm ignores the co-player’s
action.
Uncontrite and inconsistent systems fail
Knowing which consistent discriminating systems are universally stable, we can also ask precisely
what goes wrong for the rest (Fig. 4). DISC under a consistent norm is stable across all prisoner’s
dilemmas and stag hunts, yet, as already mentioned, is invaded by CDISC in snowdrift games with
2𝑆> 𝑅+ 𝑃(yielding 𝜎= 8/9). The remaining two discriminators, ODISC and PDISC, exhibit
the complementary vulnerability: under consistent norms, they are stable across all snowdrift
games and stag hunts, but invadable in prisoner’s dilemmas with 2𝑅< 𝑇+ 𝑃(again 𝜎= 8/9).
For the leading eight, the analytical results reveal which specific mutant strategies can invade and
in which game classes (SI Section 3.7). We find that all leading-eight systems are stable across the
entire class of prisoner’s dilemmas, and not merely the donation game assumed by the original
analysis3. However, when we expand the analysis to the full space of cooperative dilemmas, L2

to achieve high levels of stability in snowdrift games, stag hunts, or both (Fig. 4b).
Discussion
The moral systems that succeed under indirect reciprocity have been described in many ways –
as those that distinguish ‘justified’ from ‘unjustified’ defection3,5, or that rely on social norms
that are ‘simple and unambiguous’ and ‘in which forgiveness and repent are welcome, while
punishment is implacable’29. We have shown that what these successful systems share is moral
consistency: the social norm deems good exactly those who act as the strategy prescribes. For all
two-player cooperative dilemmas with efficient mutual cooperation, and across a wide range of
error rates, consistent discriminating systems are both highly stable and highly cooperative. The
10


underlying mechanism is easy to state: a discriminating system whose social norm is inconsistent
will award good reputations to some mutant at least as often as to its own residents, misdirecting
cooperation and becoming vulnerable to invasion. In the limit of small errors, consistent contrite
discriminating systems are the only moral systems that are universally stable. Contrition – the
readiness to cooperate when in bad standing – is what secures stability in the snowdrift games
where a consistent but uncontrite discriminator can be invaded.
Why did earlier literature not identify consistency and contrition as key ingredients of a successful
moral system? Part of the answer lies in the limited scope of earlier analyses, which considered a
restricted set of invaders, a restricted range of social dilemmas, or a restricted space of social
norms. Because each dilemma poses its own profile of invasion risks, an analysis restricted to a
subset of social dilemmas subjects moral systems to a weaker stability test: an inconsistent system
that resists invasion in one dilemma can still be invaded in another. Nakamura and Ohtsuki’s
analysis5 comes closest to ours: they surveyed the full range of dilemmas and allowed strategies
to condition on the focal player’s own reputation, the contrite discriminator among them. Yet
none of their moral systems was both stable and cooperative across dilemmas. The limitation
lay in the set of norms they explored. Because a contrite discriminator cooperates with a bad
partner only when it is itself in bad standing, a consistent norm must reward that act in a bad
player while condemning it in a good one, a distinction it can draw only by reading the focal
player’s own reputation. Nakamura and Ohtsuki’s norms conditioned on both players’ actions
and the co-player’s reputation but not the focal player’s own reputation, and so could not draw
this distinction.
Which moral systems appear successful depends on the range of dilemmas used to test them.
Consequently, the catalogues of ‘good’ norms inherited from earlier work are, to some degree,
artifacts of the kinds of social dilemma used to model social interactions. Our results thus call for
a reappraisal of the best known of these, the leading eight3,12. Once we move to the full range of
cooperative dilemmas, this set proves at once too inclusive and too exclusive. It is too inclusive
because only two of its members – L2 and L6 – are consistent; the remaining six can be invaded
once we consider social interactions beyond the prisoner’s dilemma. It is too exclusive because it
admits only the DISC and CDISC strategies, leaving out ODISC and PDISC. However, consistent
systems built on these excluded strategies are stable across all snowdrift games and stag hunts
(Fig. 4a), and as a result achieve a higher average stability index than any inconsistent member of
the leading eight – even though leading-eight systems remain more stable within the class of
prisoner’s dilemmas.
11


The consistent ODISC and PDISC systems deserve closer attention, because the behavior they
prescribe resonates with patterns seen in human societies. In particular, PDISC encodes a social
logic that evokes the phenomenon of honor cultures – societies in which retaliation for insult is
strongly socially endorsed, and in which failure to answer a slight can itself attract contempt30–32.
Honor cultures are typically organized around a mixture of direct and indirect reciprocity: people
retaliate against the particular individual who wronged them, often under public conditions in
which status is at stake. By contrast, our model is a pure case of indirect reciprocity and does not
permit targeting a specific offender. Even so, a consistent PDISC system can be read as a formal
echo of honor culture. When its standing is compromised, a PDISC individual defects regardless
of the partner’s reputation – and under its consistent norm this very defection is judged good, so
that standing is restored by adversarial display rather than by apology. Viewed through this lens,
honor cultures may not be pathologies of reciprocity but one among several consistent equilibria
in which a moral system can settle and sustain cooperation.
More broadly, our results speak to a substantial body of empirical work on human indirect
reciprocity. Controlled experiments show that people cooperate more with recipients of good
reputation, and give more generously when their own conduct is observed and may be reported to
future partners33–35; likewise, reputation shapes cooperation across a wide range of natural social
settings36. A recurring question in this literature is which social norm people actually apply when
judging others33,37–40. Our theory sharpens that question. It predicts that a population should
sustain a norm consistent with the strategy it plays, so that the standard people hold others to
coincides with the behavior they display themselves. This alignment of judgment and conduct –
and how it might break down when information becomes private or noisy – offers a target for
empirical tests of when to expect reputation-based cooperation.
Our analysis assumes that individuals apply the same moral system whatever game they happen
to be in. An alternative is to make behavior contingent on the game itself. Colnaghi et al.41
show that a costly ability to infer the structure of a social interaction and adapt to it can evolve
and promote cooperation when the mix of games is sufficiently varied. Transposed to indirect
reciprocity, this would require an entire society to detect which game it is playing and switch its
moral system in concert. For instance, a population could adopt L3 in prisoner’s dilemmas, L1 in
snowdrift games, and L5 in stag hunts (each stable within its own class, for an average stability
index of one). Yet every moral system in such a repertoire is inconsistent. Our results point to a
complementary possibility. Any consistent contrite discriminating system is stable across the
entire range of cooperative dilemmas, and thus needs no ability to identify the game and incurs
12


no cost of switching. The whole work of sustaining cooperation can thus fall to a single moral
system.
We have worked throughout with a public model of reputation, in which a single shared assessment
of each individual circulates through the population. This is a substantive assumption, relaxed
by a growing body of work asking what becomes of cooperation when observers form their
own private and sometimes conflicting opinions20–23,28,42. We adopt the public case not because
private assessment is unimportant, but because it is the cleanest setting in which to study how
social norms make reputation a reliable signal on which to condition behavior. Real populations
surely draw on both shared and private information43, and tracing how moral consistency fares
as assessment grows more private is a natural and important extension of the present work.
We have shown that the moral systems that sustain cooperation across most social dilemmas are
discriminating and consistent, and that those that prevail across every dilemma are contrite as
well. Consistency is a close cousin of the golden rule, the principle – found in some form across
many moral traditions – that we should treat others as we would have them treat us. But where
the golden rule governs conduct, consistency governs judgment: it asks us to hold others to the
standard we keep ourselves. For the contrite discriminator, consistency pairs a rule of conduct –
cooperate with everyone, but punish a bad partner when in good standing – with a matching rule
of judgment applied to others: that same punishment is deemed good in a partner, and those who
cooperate while in bad standing regain their good name. Across the entire range of cooperative
dilemmas, selection favors this alignment of judgment and conduct – moral systems that practice
what they preach, and that preach what they practice.
Methods
Model
Cooperative dilemmas.
We model social interactions as symmetric 2 × 2 games (Fig. 1a).
The action of a focal player (𝑎1) and that of their co-player (𝑎2) can be either ‘cooperate’ (𝐶)
or ‘defect’ (𝐷). We denote the payoffs for mutual cooperation, unilateral cooperation, unilateral
defection, and mutual defection respectively by 𝑅, 𝑆, 𝑇, and 𝑃.
We focus on games that constitute social dilemmas of cooperation, or ‘cooperative dilemmas’, in
the following sense24. First, actions 𝐶and 𝐷qualify as cooperation and defection: (i) mutual
cooperation is preferred over mutual defection (𝑅> 𝑃), and (ii) each player prefers their co-player
13


to cooperate rather than defect, whatever their own action (𝑅> 𝑆and 𝑇> 𝑃). Second, the game
is a genuine social dilemma: it has a (possibly mixed) evolutionarily stable strategy that does not
coincide with the social optimum – the profile maximizing total payoff – which is the case if and
only if 𝑇> 𝑅or 𝑃> 𝑆. Hence, depending on the ranking of payoff values, there are three classes
of cooperative dilemma: (i) prisoner’s dilemmas (PD) if 𝑇> 𝑅> 𝑃> 𝑆; (ii) snowdrift games
(SG, also known as chicken games) if 𝑇> 𝑅> 𝑆> 𝑃; and (iii) stag hunts (SH, also known
as assurance games) if 𝑅> 𝑇> 𝑃> 𝑆. The donation game used in most previous studies is a
special case of the prisoner’s dilemma, with 𝑅= 𝑏−𝑐, 𝑆= −𝑐, 𝑇= 𝑏, 𝑃= 0, where the benefit
of cooperation 𝑏and the cost of cooperation 𝑐satisfy 𝑏> 𝑐> 0. We further restrict attention to
cooperative dilemmas where mutual cooperation is efficient, that is, where it is itself the social
optimum; a sufficient condition for this, which we assume throughout, is 2𝑅> 𝑇+ 𝑆24.
Because our analyses are invariant under positive affine transformations of the payoffs – adding a
common constant and multiplying by a positive one – we may normalize them by the min-max
transformation
Π′ =
Π −min {𝑅, 𝑆,𝑇, 𝑃}
max {𝑅, 𝑆,𝑇, 𝑃} −min {𝑅, 𝑆,𝑇, 𝑃},
for all Π ∈{𝑅, 𝑆,𝑇, 𝑃} ,
(1)
which maps the largest payoff to one and the smallest to zero. This fixes two of the four payoffs,
leaving each cooperative dilemma class Γ ∈{PD, SG, SH} with two free parameters; each
class can then be represented as a polygon whose points are the individual dilemmas of that
class (Extended Data Fig. 2), a representation we exploit when computing stability indices (see
below). Our analysis uses the following normalized dilemmas, for which the efficiency condition
2𝑅> 𝑇+ 𝑆reduces to the constraint indicated: (i) 1 > 𝑅> 𝑃> 0 with 𝑅> 1/2 (PD); (ii)
1 > 𝑅> 𝑆> 0 with 2𝑅> 1 + 𝑆(SG); and (iii) 1 > 𝑇> 𝑃> 0 with no further constraint
(SH, where it holds automatically). The normalized donation game is the special case of the
normalized prisoner’s dilemma with 𝑃= 1 −𝑅.
Strategies and execution errors.
Strategies are used by individuals when deciding how to play
– when to cooperate with, and when to defect against, a co-player. The action of an individual
in each interaction depends on their own reputation (𝑟1) and on that of the co-player (𝑟2). With
binary reputations (‘good’= 𝐺= 1 or ‘bad’= 𝐵= 0), the strategy used by each individual
is a Boolean function 𝑝: {𝐵, 𝐺}2 →{𝐷, 𝐶} prescribing an action (‘cooperate’= 𝐶= 1 or
‘defect’= 𝐷= 0) to each combination of these two reputations. We denote each strategy by a
string 𝑝= 𝑝0𝑝1𝑝2𝑝3, where the bit 𝑝𝑖∈{𝐷, 𝐶} gives the action prescribed for one combination
14


of reputations, ordered 𝑟1 then 𝑟2 with 𝐵before 𝐺: 𝑝0 corresponds to 𝑟1 = 𝑟2 = 𝐵, 𝑝1 to 𝑟1 = 𝐵
and 𝑟2 = 𝐺, 𝑝2 to 𝑟1 = 𝐺and 𝑟2 = 𝐵, and 𝑝3 to 𝑟1 = 𝑟2 = 𝐺. Such strategies are exactly the
Boolean functions of two arguments, of which there are 222 = 24 = 16.
We model execution errors, which capture an individual’s occasional inability to carry out
an intended action. As in previous work3,5, these errors affect only intended cooperation: an
individual who intends to cooperate defects instead with probability 𝜖∈[0, 1/2) (owing, for
instance, to a lack of ability, time, or energy), whereas intended defection is always carried out.
Social norms, assignment errors, and observation model.
Social norms are used by observers
when deciding how to assign reputations to individuals after a social interaction. We model
the social norm as a Boolean function 𝑑: {𝐵, 𝐺}2 × {𝐷, 𝐶}2 →{𝐵, 𝐺} prescribing a new
reputation (‘good’= 𝐺= 1 or ‘bad’= 𝐵= 0) to each combination of the reputations and
actions of the individual being judged and their co-player. We denote each social norm by a
string 𝑑= 𝑑0𝑑1 . . . 𝑑15, where the bit 𝑑𝑖∈{𝐵, 𝐺} gives the new reputation assigned to the
judged individual for one combination of the current reputations 𝑟1, 𝑟2 and actions 𝑎1, 𝑎2 of
that individual and their co-player, ordered 𝑟1, 𝑟2, 𝑎1, 𝑎2 with 𝐵before 𝐺and 𝐷before 𝐶. For
example, 𝑑0 corresponds to 𝑟1 = 𝑟2 = 𝑎1 = 𝑎2 = 0, 𝑑5 to 𝑟1 = 𝑎1 = 0 and 𝑟2 = 𝑎2 = 1, and
𝑑15 to 𝑟1 = 𝑟2 = 𝑎1 = 𝑎2 = 1. Each social norm can equivalently be displayed as a table, as in
Figs 1c and 3a, whose rows list the reputation pairs (𝑟1, 𝑟2) = 𝐵𝐵, 𝐵𝐺, 𝐺𝐵, 𝐺𝐺from top to
bottom and whose columns list the action pairs (𝑎1, 𝑎2) = 𝐷𝐷, 𝐷𝐶, 𝐶𝐷, 𝐶𝐶from left to right.
The bitstring 𝑑0𝑑1 . . . 𝑑15 is simply this table read row by row: 𝑑0 is the top-left entry (reputations
𝐵𝐵, actions 𝐷𝐷) and 𝑑15 the bottom-right one (reputations 𝐺𝐺, actions 𝐶𝐶). Such social norms
are exactly the Boolean functions of four arguments, of which there are 224 = 216 = 65,536,
spanning different orders and classes (see Extended Data Fig. 1).
We also model assignment errors: with probability 𝜒∈(0, 1/2), the observer assigns the
reputation opposite to the one prescribed by the social norm. We adopt a ‘public-assessment
model’3,12,13,17: once an individual’s reputation is updated, it is transmitted to the whole population
(for instance, through gossip or a central authority), so that everyone agrees on the reputation of
others.
Moral systems.
A moral system (𝑝, 𝑑) pairs a strategy 𝑝with a social norm 𝑑; a population
implements it if, at all times, individuals behave according to 𝑝and observers judge according to
𝑑. Combining every strategy with every social norm yields 24 × 216 = 220 = 1,048,576 moral
systems.
15


Classes of moral systems
Discriminating strategies and systems.
Among the sixteen strategies, those that cooperate
with a good co-player and defect against a bad one when the focal player is in good standing
(𝑟1 = 𝐺) are central to our analysis. Formally, a strategy 𝑝is ‘discriminating’ if 𝑝(𝐺, 𝐵) = 𝐷
and 𝑝(𝐺, 𝐺) = 𝐶; as a string, 𝑝= ∗∗𝐷𝐶. These two fixed bits leave behavior in bad standing
unspecified, yielding the four discriminating strategies (Fig. 1b), which differ only in what
they prescribe in that case: the ‘(standard) discriminator’ (DISC, 𝑝= 𝐷𝐶𝐷𝐶) discriminates
by the co-player’s reputation just as it does when in good standing; the ‘contrite discriminator’
(CDISC, 𝑝= 𝐶𝐶𝐷𝐶) cooperates regardless of the co-player’s reputation; the ‘obfuscated
discriminator’ (ODISC, 𝑝= 𝐶𝐷𝐷𝐶) reverses the discriminating logic; and the ‘petulant
discriminator’ (PDISC, 𝑝= 𝐷𝐷𝐷𝐶) defects regardless of the co-player’s reputation. A moral
system (𝑝, 𝑑) is discriminating if its strategy 𝑝is discriminating.
Contrite strategies and systems.
A strategy 𝑝is ‘contrite’ if it cooperates whenever the
focal player is in bad standing (𝑟1 = 𝐵), that is, if 𝑝(𝐵, 𝐵) = 𝐶and 𝑝(𝐵, 𝐺) = 𝐶; as a string,
𝑝= 𝐶𝐶∗∗. Contrition thus captures the readiness to make amends – cooperating even with a bad
co-player – when one’s own reputation is bad. A moral system (𝑝, 𝑑) is contrite if its strategy
𝑝is contrite; among the four discriminating strategies, CDISC is the only contrite one, so a
discriminating system is contrite precisely when its strategy is CDISC.
Consistent systems.
A moral system (𝑝, 𝑑) is ‘consistent’ if the social norm 𝑑judges individuals
in strict accordance with the strategy 𝑝, whenever the co-player behaves as 𝑝prescribes. Formally,
(𝑝, 𝑑) is consistent if, for all 𝑟1, 𝑟2 ∈{𝐵, 𝐺}:
𝑑(𝑟1, 𝑟2, 𝑝(𝑟1, 𝑟2), 𝑝(𝑟2, 𝑟1)) = 𝐺,
(2a)
𝑑(𝑟1, 𝑟2, ¬𝑝(𝑟1, 𝑟2), 𝑝(𝑟2, 𝑟1)) = 𝐵,
(2b)
where ¬𝐶= 𝐷and ¬𝐷= 𝐶. Consistency is silent about what reputation to assign when the
co-player itself deviates from 𝑝: conditions (2) fix eight of the social norm’s sixteen bits, and
the other eight – covering exactly these deviation cases – remain free. Each strategy 𝑝therefore
gives rise to exactly 28 = 256 consistent social norms. In the absence of execution errors (𝜖= 0),
co-players – who are residents under the rare-mutant assumption – always execute the prescribed
action, so these unconstrained bits are never invoked. All 256 consistent social norms for a
given strategy therefore produce identical reputation dynamics, payoffs, and hence stability and
16


cooperation indices in this case (see SI Remark 1). A moral system that is both discriminating
and consistent is a ‘consistent discriminating system’; the four discriminating strategies therefore
give 4 × 256 = 1,024 such systems (their social norms are shown in Fig. 3a).
Evolutionary analysis
Reputation dynamics and equilibrium.
We consider an infinite, well-mixed population whose
members are repeatedly and randomly paired to play the game, with a single observer assessing
both players after each interaction (public assessment). In a resident population implementing
moral system (𝑝, 𝑑), let 𝑔𝑝,𝑑denote the fraction of individuals in good standing. An individual
with reputation 𝑟1 who meets a co-player with reputation 𝑟2 is then assigned a good reputation
with probability 𝛿𝑟1𝑟2, a function of the social norm 𝑑and the error rates 𝜖and 𝜒(SI Eq. (19)).
Averaging over the reputations of randomly matched co-players yields a one-dimensional dynamic
for 𝑔𝑝,𝑑, with a unique, asymptotically stable equilibrium 𝑔∗
𝑝,𝑑(SI Eqs. (20), (21)). All resident
quantities below are evaluated at this reputation equilibrium.
Cooperation index.
Each moral system (𝑝, 𝑑) is characterized by a cooperation index 𝜅𝑝,𝑑,
the probability that, at equilibrium, a randomly chosen resident cooperates in a social interaction.
It thus measures how cooperative the moral system is, and is independent of the game used to
model social interactions, though it depends on the error rates 𝜖and 𝜒. Concretely, 𝜅𝑝,𝑑is a
resident’s probability of cooperating, averaged over the reputations of two randomly matched
residents and evaluated at the reputation equilibrium 𝑔∗
𝑝,𝑑(SI Eq. (23)).

Numerical evaluation.
For every moral system, we test the stability condition (3) against all
15 alternative strategies. With no execution errors and in the limit of vanishing assignment errors
(𝜖= 0, 𝜒→0), the stability region SΓ
𝑝,𝑑is a polygon whose vertices we obtain analytically and
whose area we evaluate in closed form with the shoelace formula (SI Section 1.10). This yields
the exact stability indices reported in Fig. 4a,b (computed in SI Sections 3.6 and 3.7, respectively).
18


At the finite error rates used for our main results (𝜖= 𝜒= 0.02; Fig. 2), we instead estimate
each stability index by Monte Carlo integration, drawing 1,000 payoff configurations uniformly
at random from the normalized region GΓ of each dilemma class and recording the fraction of
configurations at which 𝑝is stable under 𝑑. Because a moral system and its ‘mirror’ – the system
obtained by swapping the two reputation labels throughout – are behaviorally equivalent, we
evaluate one representative per mirror class, 524,800 in total (SI Sections 4.1 and 4.2).
Righteous and universally stable systems.
Our central analytical results concern several
further properties of moral systems, all evaluated in a tractable regime: no execution errors (𝜖= 0)
and the limit of vanishing assignment errors (𝜒→0). A moral system is ‘self-approving’ if almost
all of its residents attain good standing, that is, if the resident reputation equilibrium approaches
one, lim𝜒→0(𝑔∗
𝑝,𝑑)𝜖=0 = 1 (SI Definition 8). It is ‘self-cooperative’ if its residents cooperate at
near-maximal rates, that is, if the cooperation index approaches one, lim𝜒→0(𝜅𝑝,𝑑)𝜖=0 = 1 (SI
Definition 10). It is ‘righteous’ if it is both self-approving and self-cooperative (SI Definition 11).
Finally, it is ‘universally stable’ if it is stable across every cooperative dilemma we consider, that
is, if its stability index approaches one in each of the three classes, lim𝜒→0(𝜎Γ
𝑝,𝑑)𝜖=0 = 1 for all
Γ ∈{PD, SG, SH} (SI Definition 12).
Data availability
The archived payoff samples used for the Monte Carlo stability estimates are distributed with
the code (see Code availability). The computed cooperation and stability indices for all moral
systems and error-rate combinations, from which the figures are generated (SI Section 4.2), are
deposited at https://doi.org/10.26180/33068042.
Code availability
The Julia package MoralConsistency, which implements the reputation dynamics, fitness
computations, and stability tests, together with the Python scripts that generate the figures, is
openly available at https://github.com/ghostleopold/moral-consistency-reproducibility and
permanently archived at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.21519395.

Extended Data Fig. 3: How CDISC can invade a consistent DISC system. CDISC and DISC
differ only in the rare, error-induced state where both players have bad reputations. Pawns carry
their reputations (𝐺or 𝐵) and follow the strategies shown beside them (Fig. 1b); grey letters
give actions, and blue annotations the focal player’s payoffs. Each round, an observer (blue
pawn) judges both players by a DISC-consistent social norm (∗marks entries unconstrained
by consistency; Fig. 3a) and broadcasts the verdicts. a, Trajectory of a mutant CDISC (orange)
starting in bad standing. At 𝑡= 1, the mutant meets a resident also in bad standing and contritely
cooperates, earning 𝑆; because cooperating with a bad partner deviates from DISC’s prescription,
it is judged bad (the resident’s verdict is unconstrained, hence ‘GOOD/BAD’). At 𝑡= 2, it meets
another resident, almost surely in good standing, and cooperates, matching DISC’s prescription;
it is judged good but its co-player defects, so it earns 𝑆again. Its reputation is restored only after
two rounds, for a total payoff of 2𝑆. b, Trajectory of a resident DISC in the analogous situation.
At 𝑡= 1, it defects, as DISC prescribes, earns 𝑃, and is judged good; at 𝑡= 2, in good standing
like almost all residents, it cooperates and earns 𝑅, for a total of 𝑃+ 𝑅. CDISC thus gains a net
advantage, and can invade, whenever 2𝑆> 𝑅+ 𝑃, which only snowdrift games satisfy.

study: DISC never gains an advantage and cannot invade a consistent CDISC system.

2


1
Model
This section provides a detailed description of the model used in the main text. We consider an
infinite, well-mixed population whose members interact in pairwise encounters, each modeled as
a symmetric two-player game. Each game belongs to one of three classes of cooperative dilemmas
(prisoner’s dilemmas, snowdrift games, or stag hunts); throughout we restrict attention to those
for which mutual cooperation is the social optimum, an efficiency condition on the payoffs that
we make precise below. Individuals choose their actions according to a strategy (or action rule)
that conditions on the binary reputations of the focal player and their co-player, while an observer
updates both players’ reputations according to an assessment rule (or social norm) that conditions
additionally on the two actions taken. Sections 1.1–1.3 establish labeling conventions and define
the payoff function and the three classes of cooperative dilemmas. Section 1.4 introduces notation
for strategies and assessment rules, and Section 1.5 counts assessment rules by the arguments
they use, situating the space of social norms we explore relative to those studied in previous work.
Section 1.6 describes the reputation dynamics of a resident population, from which Section 1.7
derives the cooperation index; Section 1.8 extends the reputation dynamics to rare mutants, and
Section 1.9 derives the equilibrium payoffs and defines evolutionary stability. Finally, Section 1.10
defines the stability indices we use to assess the evolutionary robustness of moral systems across
the full space of cooperative dilemmas.
1.1
Labeling conventions for actions and reputations
Actions and reputations are binary. In each interaction, an individual either ‘cooperates’ (C) or
‘defects’ (D); after the interaction, an observer may assign the individual a ‘good’ (G) or a ‘bad’
(B) reputation. For notational convenience, we identify cooperation and a good reputation with 1,
and defection and a bad reputation with 0:
C = G = 1, and
(1a)
D = B = 0.
(1b)
We also use the wildcard symbol ∗to denote a placeholder that may represent either 0 (D or B) or
1 (C or G).
1.2
Payoff function
Social interactions are modeled as symmetric 2 × 2 games. A focal player choosing action
𝑎1 ∈{D, C} against a co-player choosing action 𝑎2 ∈{D, C} receives a payoff 𝑣(𝑎1, 𝑎2), where
𝑣: {D, C}2 →R. Writing the focal player’s action as the row and the co-player’s as the column,
this payoff function takes the matrix form

C
D
C
𝑣(C, C)
𝑣(C, D)
D
𝑣(D, C)
𝑣(D, D)

.
(2)
Identifying
𝑣(C, C) = 𝑅, 𝑣(C, D) = 𝑆, 𝑣(D, C) = 𝑇, and 𝑣(D, D) = 𝑃,
we recover the formulation given in the main text (Fig. 1a; see also Methods).
Throughout, we also use the shorthand notation:
v𝑎1𝑎2 = 𝑣(𝑎1, 𝑎2).
3


1.3
Cooperative dilemmas and payoff spaces
We focus on the generic 2 × 2 games that constitute cooperative dilemmas in which mutual
cooperation is the social optimum1. This requires that all of the following inequalities hold:
𝑅> 𝑃,
(3)
𝑅> 𝑆,
(4)
𝑇> 𝑃,
(5)
2𝑅> 𝑇+ 𝑆,
(6)
and that either
𝑇> 𝑅
(7)
or
𝑃> 𝑆
(8)
also holds. Inequality (3) means that mutual cooperation is preferred over mutual defection.
Inequalities (4) and (5) imply that each player prefers the co-player to cooperate rather than defect.
Inequality (6) ensures that two cooperators earn a higher total payoff (2𝑅) than a pair in which
one cooperates and the other defects (𝑇+ 𝑆); together with (3), this makes mutual cooperation
the social optimum. Inequalities (7) and (8) indicate that there is some temptation for players to
defect; this is a necessary condition for the game to be a social dilemma1.
Three classes of games fulfill these requirements1: prisoner’s dilemmas (PD), characterized
by
𝑇> 𝑅> 𝑃> 𝑆and 2𝑅> 𝑇+ 𝑆;
(9)
snowdrift games (SG), characterized by
𝑇> 𝑅> 𝑆> 𝑃and 2𝑅> 𝑇+ 𝑆;
(10)
and stag hunts (SH), characterized by
𝑅> 𝑇> 𝑃> 𝑆,
(11)
which trivially satisfy (6). Note that all three classes satisfy
𝑇> 𝑆.
(12)
Normalizing the payoffs by a min-max transformation (Methods), so that the largest payoff
equals one and the smallest equals zero, we obtain
1 > 𝑅> 𝑃> 0 and 2𝑅> 1
(13)
for prisoner’s dilemmas,
1 > 𝑅> 𝑆> 0 and 2𝑅> 1 + 𝑆
(14)
for snowdrift games, and
1 > 𝑇> 𝑃> 0
(15)
for stag hunts. This fixes two of the four payoffs, leaving each class with two free parameters (see
Extended Data Fig. 2 of the main text).

1.4
Strategies, assessment rules, and shorthand notations
Consider a population where observers judge players according to assessment rule 𝑑, resident
players adopt strategy 𝑝, and a small fraction of mutant players adopt strategy ˜𝑝. As specified in
the Methods of the main text, the strategies 𝑝and ˜𝑝are Boolean functions 𝑝, ˜𝑝: {B, G}2 →{D, C}
prescribing an action for each combination of the reputations of the focal player and their co-player.
Likewise, the assessment rule is a Boolean function 𝑑: {B, G}2 × {D, C}2 →{B, G} prescribing
a reputation for each combination of the reputations and actions of the focal player and their
co-player.
Consider a focal player who interacts with a co-player and is judged by an observer. Let
𝑟1 ∈{B, G} denote the reputation of the focal player and 𝑟2 ∈{B, G} that of the co-player. The
focal player’s strategy prescribes action 𝑝(𝑟1, 𝑟2) if they are a resident and ˜𝑝(𝑟1, 𝑟2) if they are a
mutant. Once the focal player has taken an action 𝑎1 ∈{D, C} against a co-player who has played
𝑎2 ∈{D, C}, the assessment rule prescribes a new reputation 𝑑(𝑟1, 𝑟2, 𝑎1, 𝑎2) for the focal player.
It is convenient to introduce shorthand notation for the actions and reputations prescribed in
different situations. We use the following conventions throughout:
p𝑟1𝑟2 := 𝑝(𝑟1, 𝑟2),
(16a)
˜p𝑟1𝑟2 := ˜𝑝(𝑟1, 𝑟2),
(16b)
d𝑟1𝑟2𝑎1𝑎2 := 𝑑(𝑟1, 𝑟2, 𝑎1, 𝑎2),
(16c)
d𝑟1𝑟2 := 𝑑(𝑟1, 𝑟2, p𝑟1𝑟2, p𝑟2𝑟1),
(16d)
˜d𝑟1𝑟2 := 𝑑(𝑟1, 𝑟2, ˜p𝑟1𝑟2, p𝑟2𝑟1),
(16e)
¯d𝑟1𝑟2 := 𝑑(𝑟1, 𝑟2, ¬p𝑟1𝑟2, p𝑟2𝑟1),
(16f)
where ¬ stands for the logical negation (with our labeling convention (1), ¬C = D and ¬D = C). In
expressions (16d)–(16f) the co-player is a resident who behaves as prescribed, playing 𝑝(𝑟2, 𝑟1);
the three differ only in the focal player’s action. Expression (16d) is the new reputation prescribed
to a resident focal player, who plays 𝑝(𝑟1, 𝑟2), and (16e) that prescribed to a mutant focal
player, who plays ˜𝑝(𝑟1, 𝑟2) – both in the absence of execution errors. Expression (16f) is the
new reputation prescribed to a focal player (resident or mutant) whose action deviates from the
resident prescription, playing ¬𝑝(𝑟1, 𝑟2).
1.5
Counting assessment rules by the arguments they use
Extended Data Fig. 1 of the main text situates the space of assessment rules we study relative to
the spaces explored in previous work. Here we define the sets appearing there and derive their
cardinalities.
An assessment rule is a Boolean function of the four binary arguments in V = {𝑟1, 𝑟2, 𝑎1, 𝑎2}.
For a subset U ⊆V, let TU denote the set of assessment rules of type U: those that can be
written as functions of the arguments in U alone. There are |TU| = 22|U| rules of type U, and
TU ∩TU′ = TU∩U′, since a rule that ignores the arguments outside U and the arguments outside
U′ ignores all arguments outside U ∩U′. For 𝑘= 0, 1, . . . , 4, let
𝑂𝑘:=

denote the set of assessment rules of order at most 𝑘; in particular, 𝑂4 is the universe of
224 = 216 = 65,536 assessment rules studied in this paper.

A rule is said to depend essentially on the subset U if it is of type U but not of type U′ for
any proper subset U′ ⊂U. The following proposition counts assessment rules by their essential
arguments and, with them, the sets 𝑂𝑘.
Proposition 1 (Number of assessment rules of each order). For 𝑘= 0, 1, . . . , 4, the number of
assessment rules that depend essentially on a given subset U ⊆V with 𝑘elements depends only
on 𝑘. Denoting this number by 𝑁𝑘, it satisfies the recursion

Proof. Since type classes are closed under intersection, each rule is of a unique minimal type,
that is, it depends essentially on exactly one subset of V. Consequently, the 22|U| rules of type U
partition according to the subset U′ ⊆U on which they depend essentially, so the number 𝐸(U)
of rules depending essentially on U satisfies 𝐸(U) = 22|U| −Í
U′⊊U 𝐸(U′). By induction
on |U|, starting from 𝐸(∅) = 2 (the two constant rules, which assign G to everybody or B to
everybody), 𝐸(U) depends only on 𝑘= |U|, and grouping the proper subsets of U by their
cardinalities yields the recursion (17). Finally, a rule belongs to 𝑂𝑘if and only if the subset on
which it depends essentially has at most 𝑘elements; summing 𝑁𝑗over the  4

𝑗≤𝑘elements yields Eq. (18).
□
The spaces explored by previous literature are T{𝑎1,𝑟1,𝑟2}, the 223 = 256 third-order rules
of type 𝑎1𝑟1𝑟2 2, and T{𝑎1,𝑎2,𝑟2}, the 256 third-order rules of type 𝑎1𝑎2𝑟2 3. By the intersection
property above, a rule belongs to both spaces if and only if it is of type 𝑎1𝑟2. The two spaces
therefore share |T{𝑎1,𝑟2}| = 222 = 16 rules, and their union contains 256 + 256 −16 = 496 rules.
Consider first the |𝑂2| = 70 rules of order at most two (Proposition 1). Such a rule lies in
T{𝑎1,𝑟1,𝑟2} whenever its essential arguments avoid 𝑎2, and in T{𝑎1,𝑎2,𝑟2} whenever they avoid 𝑟1.
The rules of order at most two lying outside both previously studied spaces are therefore exactly
the rules that depend essentially on the pair 𝑎2𝑟1, which number 𝑁2 = 10. The rules of order at
most two belonging to T{𝑎1,𝑟1,𝑟2} are those whose essential arguments form one of the 1 + 3 + 3
subsets of {𝑎1, 𝑟1, 𝑟2} with at most two elements; they number 𝑁0 + 3𝑁1 + 3𝑁2 = 38. Of these,
the 16 rules of type 𝑎1𝑟2 are shared with T{𝑎1,𝑎2,𝑟2}, leaving 38 −16 = 22 exclusive ones; by
symmetry, the same counts hold for T{𝑎1,𝑎2,𝑟2}.
Consider next the rules of essential order three. Those depending essentially on all of 𝑎1,
𝑟1, 𝑟2 number 𝑁3 = 218 and complete the count 38 + 218 = 256 of T{𝑎1,𝑟1,𝑟2}; symmetrically
for T{𝑎1,𝑎2,𝑟2}. The two remaining triples define the type classes T{𝑎1,𝑎2,𝑟1} and T{𝑎2,𝑟1,𝑟2}, which
contain the pair 𝑎2𝑟1 and were not previously explored. Their 2𝑁3 = 436 essentially third-
order rules, together with the 10 rules that depend essentially on the pair 𝑎2𝑟1, account for all
|𝑂3| −496 = 446 rules of order at most three lying outside the previously studied spaces. The
remaining 𝑁4 = 64,594 rules of the universe 𝑂4 are of essential order four.
All of these counts were additionally verified by exhaustive enumeration of the 216 assessment
rules. In Extended Data Fig. 1, the area of every region is proportional, within each panel, to the
number of assessment rules it contains.

1.6
Resident reputation dynamics and equilibrium
Throughout, we adopt a public-assessment model of reputation2,4: a single observer witnesses
each interaction and assigns each of the two players a new reputation, and these assessments
are then shared with the whole population (for instance, through gossip or a central authority),
so that every individual holds the same view of any given player. This shared, population-wide
agreement is what allows the population’s state to be summarized by a single variable – the
fraction of individuals in good standing – whose dynamics we now derive.
Consider a resident population implementing moral system (𝑝, 𝑑): all players behave according
to strategy 𝑝and observers judge according to assessment rule 𝑑. Within this population, take
a focal player with reputation 𝑟1 ∈{B, G} playing a game against a co-player with reputation
𝑟2 ∈{B, G}; the resident strategy then prescribes that the focal player play p𝑟1𝑟2 and the co-player
play p𝑟2𝑟1. Given our labeling convention (1), the probability that the focal player is assigned a
good reputation can be written as

2
Definitions and preliminary results
Here we introduce terminology for the subsets of strategies and moral systems that recur in
our analysis. These definitions provide the conceptual scaffolding for the theoretical results of
subsequent sections.
In Section 2.1, we distinguish strategies according to whether they respond to reputation
information. Unconditional strategies (ALLC and ALLD) prescribe the same action regardless
of the two players’ reputations, whereas conditional strategies allow behavior to depend on this
information. Among conditional strategies, we single out the discriminating strategies, which
cooperate with co-players regarded as good and defect against those regarded as bad (provided the
focal player is in good standing), and the contrite strategies, which cooperate whenever the focal
player is in bad standing. We extend these notions to moral systems (𝑝, 𝑑), defining conditional,
discriminating, and contrite systems accordingly.
In Section 2.2, we characterize moral systems by their equilibrium properties at the population
level. A moral system is self-approving if a resident population comes to regard itself as good,
12


and self-cooperative if it sustains mutual cooperation, both in the absence of execution errors
and the limit of vanishing assignment errors. Combining these, a system is righteous if it is both
self-approving and self-cooperative. Finally, a system is universally stable if it resists invasion by
any alternative strategy across all three classes of cooperative dilemma.
In Section 2.3, we introduce consistent systems, in which the assessment rule endorses as
good exactly those who follow the action rule and penalizes those who deviate from it. Consistent
discriminating systems are central to our analysis; we describe their four subsets, one for each
discriminating strategy.
In Section 2.4, we define the leading-eight moral systems of Ohtsuki and Iwasa2, which
serve as reference points throughout our analysis. Finally, in Section 2.5, we formalize the mirror
operation – the swapping of the two reputation labels – and prove the preliminary results used in
later sections: a moral system and its mirror are behaviorally equivalent, whereas label-dependent
properties such as self-approval hold for at most one member of each mirror pair.
2.1
Conditional, discriminating, and contrite strategies and systems
We begin by defining unconditional and conditional strategies.
Definition 1 (Unconditional strategies). A strategy is said to be unconditional if pBB = pBG =
pGB = pGG holds.
There are exactly two unconditional strategies in our strategy space: the ‘unconditional
defector’ (ALLD), with pBB = pBG = pGB = pGG = D, and the ‘unconditional cooperator’ (ALLC),
with pBB = pBG = pGB = pGG = C.
Definition 2 (Conditional strategies). A strategy is said to be conditional if it is not unconditional.
Among conditional strategies, a particularly important subset consists of those that, when the
focal player is in good standing (𝑟1 = G), prescribe cooperation toward co-players regarded as
good (𝑟2 = G) and defection against those regarded as bad (𝑟2 = B). We define these strategies
formally as follows.
Definition 3 (Discriminating strategies). A strategy 𝑝is said to be discriminating if both
pGB = D and pGG = C
hold.
As a string, discriminating strategies take the form 𝑝= **DC. Depending on what they
prescribe when the focal player is in bad standing, we distinguish four discriminating strategies:
1. ‘(Standard) discriminator’ (DISC, 𝑝= DCDC), which, when in bad standing, discriminates
by the co-player’s reputation just as in good standing.
2. ‘Contrite discriminator’ (CDISC, 𝑝= CCDC), which, when in bad standing, cooperates
regardless of the co-player’s reputation.
3. ‘Obfuscated discriminator’ (ODISC, 𝑝= CDDC), which, when in bad standing, reverses the
discriminating logic, cooperating with bad co-players and defecting against good ones.
4. ‘Petulant discriminator’ (PDISC, 𝑝= DDDC), which, when in bad standing, defects regardless
of the co-player’s reputation.

Table 1: The 16 strategies (action rules). Each strategy is a Boolean function written as a bitstring
𝑝= pBBpBGpGBpGG in the order BB, BG, GB, GG; its ID is this bitstring read as a little-endian binary
number (C = G = 1, D = B = 0, with BB the least significant bit). The last three columns indicate
whether the strategy is conditional, discriminating, and contrite (✓) or not (✗).
A further property central to our analysis concerns strategies that prescribe cooperation
whenever the focal player is in bad standing (𝑟1 = B), a readiness to make amends that we call
contrition.
Definition 4 (Contrite strategies). A strategy 𝑝is said to be contrite if both
pBB = C and pBG = C
hold.
As a string, contrite strategies take the form 𝑝= CC**. Among the four discriminating
strategies, CDISC (𝑝= CCDC) is the only one that is also contrite; it is thus the unique contrite
discriminator.
All 24 = 16 strategies, along with their conditional, discriminating, and contrite status, are
shown in Table 1.
Our general goal is to identify moral systems (𝑝, 𝑑) that sustain high levels of cooperation
(as measured by the cooperation index) and resist invasion by every alternative strategy ˜𝑝≠𝑝.
Neither unconditional strategy yields such a system, whatever the assessment rule 𝑑: ALLD
(𝑝= DDDD) sustains no cooperation, since individuals following it never cooperate, while ALLC
(𝑝= CCCC) is invaded by ALLD in prisoner’s dilemmas and snowdrift games1. For these reasons,
we focus on moral systems implementing a conditional strategy.
Definition 5 (Conditional systems). A moral system (𝑝, 𝑑) is said to be conditional if its action
rule 𝑝is conditional.
We denote the set of conditional systems by C. Explicitly,
C = {(𝑝, 𝑑) |𝑝∉{ALLC, ALLD}} .
14


Since conditional systems implementing a discriminating strategy play a central role in our
analysis, we single them out with the following definition.
Definition 6 (Discriminating systems). A moral system (𝑝, 𝑑) is said to be discriminating if its
action rule 𝑝is discriminating.
We denote the set of discriminating systems by D. Explicitly,
D = {(𝑝, 𝑑) |𝑝∈{DISC, CDISC, ODISC, PDISC}} .
Analogously, we single out systems whose action rule is contrite.
Definition 7 (Contrite systems). A moral system (𝑝, 𝑑) is said to be contrite if its action rule 𝑝is
contrite.
Since CDISC is the unique contrite discriminating strategy, a discriminating system is contrite
if and only if its action rule is CDISC.

2.3
Consistent systems and consistent discriminating systems
2.3.1
Consistent systems
Consistency captures the idea that a moral system’s action rule and assessment rule do not
contradict each other: following the prescribed behavior is rewarded with a good reputation, and
deviating from it is punished with a bad one.
Definition 13 (Consistent systems). A conditional system (𝑝, 𝑑) is said to be consistent if both
dBB = dBG = dGB = dGG = G, and
(43a)
¯dBB = ¯dBG = ¯dGB = ¯dGG = B
(43b)
hold. If (𝑝, 𝑑) is consistent, we also say that 𝑝is consistent with 𝑑, or that 𝑑is consistent with 𝑝.
We denote the set of consistent systems by Q. Thus,
Q =

(𝑝, 𝑑)
(𝑝, 𝑑) ∈C, d𝑟1𝑟2 = G, and ¯d𝑟1𝑟2 = B for all 𝑟1, 𝑟2 ∈{B, G}
	
.
Recall our notational conventions for d𝑟1𝑟2 (16d) and ¯d𝑟1𝑟2 (16f). Conditions (43a) and (43b)
together say that, whenever the co-player plays according to action rule 𝑝, assessment rule 𝑑
regards the focal player as good if they follow 𝑝’s prescription and as bad if they deviate from it.
The moral system (𝑝, 𝑑) is then consistent in the sense that there is no contradiction between the
behavior prescribed by the action rule and the moral judgment expressed by the assessment rule.
For each strategy 𝑝, the two conditions in Eq. (43) specify the values of eight out of the sixteen
bits of an assessment rule that is consistent with 𝑝. Once these values are set, the remaining eight
bits can be set to any binary value, and 𝑑will still be consistent with 𝑝, according to Definition
13. It follows that each strategy 𝑝gives rise to 28 = 256 consistent systems (𝑝, 𝑑).
Remark 1 (Equivalence of consistent systems at 𝜖= 0). The eight unconstrained bits of a
consistent assessment rule are precisely those of the form 𝑑(𝑟1, 𝑟2, 𝑎1, ¬𝑝(𝑟2, 𝑟1)) for 𝑟1, 𝑟2 ∈
{B, G} and 𝑎1 ∈{D, C} – that is, the bits specifying how to assess a focal player when the co-
player’s action deviates from the resident prescription. In the reputation transition probability (19),
these bits appear only in the terms proportional to 𝜖(1 −𝜖) and 𝜖2, which vanish when
𝜖= 0. The same holds for the mutant reputation transition (24): at 𝜖= 0, the surviving term
𝑑(𝑟1, 𝑟2, ˜𝑝(𝑟1, 𝑟2), 𝑝(𝑟2, 𝑟1)) involves a constrained bit regardless of whether the mutant’s action
agrees with the resident’s, since conditions (43a) and (43b) together fix 𝑑(𝑟1, 𝑟2, 𝑎1, 𝑝(𝑟2, 𝑟1))
for both values of 𝑎1. Consequently, all 256 consistent systems sharing the same action rule 𝑝
have identical resident equilibria 𝑔∗
𝑝,𝑑, mutant equilibria ℎ∗
˜𝑝,𝑝,𝑑, payoffs, and hence stability and
cooperation indices, whenever 𝜖= 0.

Figure 3: Social norms of the leading-eight moral systems, arranged by group. Group I systems
(L1 and L2) use CDISC as their action rule; Group II (L3–L6) and Group III (L7 and L8) systems
use DISC.
Conditions (48a) and (48b) state that, irrespective of the focal player’s reputation, cooperation
with good co-players is judged as good and defection against them as bad. Condition (48c) states
that defection against bad co-players by good individuals is judged as good, encapsulating the
concept of ‘justified defection’2. The leading-eight moral systems are named and described in
Fig. 3.
The leading-eight moral systems differ in two respects: (i) whether they use DISC or CDISC
as their action rule, and (ii) their assignment of the bits dBBD∗, dBBC∗, and dGBC∗, which govern how
interactions involving bad co-players are judged. In line with the original analysis by Ohtsuki and
Iwasa2, we distinguish the following three groups.
Group I.
Group I systems use 𝑝= CCDC (i.e. CDISC), with dBBD∗= B and dBBC∗= G. We
denote this set by LI:
LI = {(𝑝, 𝑑) |𝑝= CCDC, dBBD∗= dBGD∗= dGGD∗= B,
dBBC∗= dBGC∗= dGBD∗= dGGC∗= G, and dGBCD = dGBCC} .
(49)
19


The two members are L1 (CDISC, Standing), with dGBC∗= G, and L2 (CDISC, Consistent
Standing), with dGBC∗= B (see Fig. 3):
L1 = {(𝑝, 𝑑) |𝑝= CCDC, dBBD∗= dBGD∗= dGGD∗= B,
and dBBC∗= dBGC∗= dGBD∗= dGBC∗= dGGC∗= G} ,
(50)
L2 = {(𝑝, 𝑑) |𝑝= CCDC, dBBD∗= dBGD∗= dGBC∗= dGGD∗= B,
and dBBC∗= dBGC∗= dGBD∗= dGGC∗= G} .
(51)
Note that L2 belongs to QCDISC (cf. Eqs. (51) and (45)).
Group II.
Group II systems use 𝑝= DCDC (i.e. DISC), with dBBD∗= G. We denote this set by
LII:
LII = {(𝑝, 𝑑) |𝑝= DCDC, dBGD∗= dGGD∗= B,
dBBD∗= dBGC∗= dGBD∗= dGGC∗= G,
dBBCD = dBBCC, and dGBCD = dGBCC} .
(52)
The four members are L3 (DISC, Simple Standing), with dBBC∗= dGBC∗= G; L4 (DISC, SS-SJ),
with dBBC∗= B and dGBC∗= G; L5 (DISC, SJ-SS), with dBBC∗= G and dGBC∗= B; and L6 (DISC,
Stern Judging), with dBBC∗= dGBC∗= B (see Fig. 3):
L3 = {(𝑝, 𝑑) |𝑝= DCDC, dBGD∗= dGGD∗= B,
and dBBD∗= dBBC∗= dBGC∗= dGBD∗= dGBC∗= dGGC∗= G} ,
(53)
L4 = {(𝑝, 𝑑) |𝑝= DCDC, dBBC∗= dBGD∗= dGGD∗= B,
and dBBD∗= dBGC∗= dGBD∗= dGBC∗= dGGC∗= G} ,
(54)
L5 = {(𝑝, 𝑑) |𝑝= DCDC, dBGD∗= dGBC∗= dGGD∗= B,
and dBBC∗= dBBD∗= dBGC∗= dGBD∗= dGGC∗= G} ,
(55)
L6 = {(𝑝, 𝑑) |𝑝= DCDC, dBBC∗= dBGD∗= dGBC∗= dGGD∗= B,
and dBBD∗= dBGC∗= dGBD∗= dGGC∗= G} .
(56)
Note that L6 belongs to QDISC (cf. Eqs. (56) and (44)).
Group III.
Group III systems use 𝑝= DCDC (i.e. DISC), with dBBD∗= dBBC∗= B. We denote
this set by LIII:
LIII = {(𝑝, 𝑑) |𝑝= DCDC, dBBD∗= dBBC∗= dBGD∗= dGGD∗= B,
dBGC∗= dGBD∗= dGGC∗= G, and dGBCD = dGBCC} .
(57)
The two members are L7 (DISC, Staying), with dGBC∗= G, and L8 (DISC, Judging), with
dGBC∗= B (see Fig. 3):
L7 = {(𝑝, 𝑑) |𝑝= DCDC, dBBD∗= dBBC∗= dBGD∗= dGGD∗= B,
and dBGC∗= dGBD∗= dGBC∗= dGGC∗= G} ,
(58)
L8 = {(𝑝, 𝑑) |𝑝= DCDC, dBBD∗= dBBC∗= dBGD∗= dGBC∗= dGGD∗= B,
and dBGC∗= dGBD∗= dGGC∗= G} .
(59)
We denote by L the set of all leading-eight moral systems, so that
L = LI ∪LII ∪LIII.

2.5
Mirror systems
The reputation labels G (‘good’) and B (‘bad’) are arbitrary bookkeeping devices: unlike actions,
which determine payoffs through the payoff function 𝑣, reputations have no intrinsic consequences.
Swapping the two labels throughout a moral system therefore yields a system that is behaviorally
indistinguishable from the original – an observation made by Ohtsuki and Iwasa2 for the
third-order assessment rules they studied, and which they termed ‘mirror symmetry’ (their
Appendix A). This subsection formalizes this relabeling operation and verifies that the quantities
of interest (reputation equilibria, cooperation index, stability indices) are invariant under it;
Section 4.1 builds on these results to count moral systems up to relabeling and to specify the
representatives evaluated in our numerical explorations.
Definition 15 (Mirror systems). The mirror of a moral system (𝑝, 𝑑) is the moral system ( ˆ𝑝, ˆ𝑑)
given by
ˆ𝑝(𝑟1, 𝑟2) = 𝑝(¬𝑟1, ¬𝑟2),
(61a)
ˆ𝑑(𝑟1, 𝑟2, 𝑎1, 𝑎2) = ¬ 𝑑(¬𝑟1, ¬𝑟2, 𝑎1, 𝑎2),
(61b)
for all 𝑟1, 𝑟2 ∈{B, G} and 𝑎1, 𝑎2 ∈{D, C}, where ¬ denotes the swap B ↔G (equivalently, with
our labeling convention (1), ¬𝑥= 1 −𝑥). A moral system that is equal to its mirror is said to be a
self-mirror.
The mirror relabels reputations wherever they occur – in the reputation arguments of 𝑝and 𝑑,
and in the reputation prescribed by 𝑑– while leaving actions untouched. As a bitstring (Table 1),
ˆ𝑝is simply 𝑝read from right to left, so that, for instance, the mirror of DISC (𝑝= DCDC) is
the strategy CDCD, which cooperates with co-players in bad standing. Applying the mirror twice
recovers the original system, so the mirror map is an involution on the space of moral systems: it
partitions this space into ‘mirror classes’ of size one (self-mirrors) or two (‘mirror pairs’).
The next lemma shows that the mirror system reproduces the reputation dynamics of the
original system with the roles of G and B interchanged.
Lemma 3 (Reputation dynamics of mirror systems). Let ( ˆ𝑝, ˆ𝑑) be the mirror of (𝑝, 𝑑), and let ˆ˜𝑝
be the mirror of a mutant strategy ˜𝑝. Denote by ˆ𝛿𝑟1𝑟2 the probability (19) that a resident of a
population implementing ( ˆ𝑝, ˆ𝑑) is assigned a good reputation, and by ˆ˜𝛿𝑟1𝑟2 the corresponding
probability (24) for a mutant adopting ˆ˜𝑝in this population. Then, for all error rates 𝜒∈(0, 1/2)
and 𝜖∈[0, 1/2), and for all 𝑟1, 𝑟2 ∈{B, G},
ˆ𝛿𝑟1𝑟2 = 1 −𝛿¬𝑟1 ¬𝑟2
and
ˆ˜𝛿𝑟1𝑟2 = 1 −˜𝛿¬𝑟1 ¬𝑟2.
(62)
Consequently, the resident and mutant reputation equilibria satisfy

With Lemma 3 in place, the behavioral equivalence of a moral system and its mirror follows
readily.

Whereas quantities defined from behavior alone are mirror-invariant, properties whose
definitions refer to the reputation labels are not. The following corollary records the consequences
for the classes of moral systems defined above.
Corollary 1 (Mirrors and classes of moral systems). Let ( ˆ𝑝, ˆ𝑑) be the mirror of a conditional
system (𝑝, 𝑑). Then:
(i) (𝑝, 𝑑) is self-approving if and only if ( ˆ𝑝, ˆ𝑑) is self-disapproving; in particular, the mirror
map restricts to a bijection A →Z, so |A| = |Z|.
22


(ii) (𝑝, 𝑑) is self-cooperative (resp. universally stable) if and only if ( ˆ𝑝, ˆ𝑑) is self-cooperative
(resp. universally stable).
(iii) Every self-mirror satisfies 𝑔∗
𝑝,𝑑= 1/2 for all error rates 𝜒∈(0, 1/2) and 𝜖∈[0, 1/2); in
particular, no self-mirror is self-approving or self-disapproving, and no righteous system
is a self-mirror.
Proof. Claims (i) and (ii) follow from Lemma 3 and Proposition 2 by taking the limits in
Definitions 8, 9, 10, and 12 (the mirror of a conditional system is conditional, since ˆ𝑝is
unconditional if and only if 𝑝is). For (iii), a self-mirror satisfies 𝑔∗
𝑝,𝑑= 𝑔∗

𝑝,𝑑= 1/2; a righteous system is self-approving by Definition 11, so it cannot be a
self-mirror.
□
3
Analytical results
This section derives the analytical results that underpin the main text’s central claims. We
proceed in two stages. The first stage characterizes the structural properties of moral systems.
Sections 3.1–3.3 identify necessary and sufficient conditions for a system to be self-approving,
self-cooperative, righteous, consistent, or consistent discriminating, and establish the inclusion
relations among these sets. Section 3.4 then verifies that the leading-eight moral systems fall into
the appropriate sets in this hierarchy. The second stage turns to evolutionary stability. Section 3.5
identifies which righteous systems are universally stable, establishing that universal stability is
equivalent to being a contrite consistent discriminating system. Sections 3.6 and 3.7 then provide
detailed stability analyses for all consistent discriminating systems (grouped by action rule) and
for each of the leading-eight moral systems individually. Throughout, we focus on self-approving
systems; self-disapproving systems are behaviorally equivalent under the mirror operation of
Section 2.5 and require no separate treatment.
3.1
Characterization of self-approving systems
Here, we characterize the sets of self-approving and self-disapproving moral systems. We begin
with the following result, which provides simple necessary and sufficient conditions for a moral
system to be self-approving (or self-disapproving).
Proposition 3 (Characterization of self-approving and self-disapproving systems). A conditional
system (𝑝, 𝑑) is self-approving if and only if
(i) dGG = G, and
(ii) dBB + dBG + dGB ≥2, i.e., at least two of {dBB, dBG, dGB} are G.
Similarly, a conditional system (𝑝, 𝑑) is self-disapproving if and only if
(i) dBB = B, and
(ii) dBG + dGB + dGG ≤1, i.e., at least two of {dBG, dGB, dGG} are B.
23


Proof. To identify the set of self-approving systems for which condition (40) in Definition 8

𝑎(𝜒) = (1 −2𝜒) [dBB + dGG −dBG −dGB] ,

𝑐(𝜒) = 𝜒+ (1 −2𝜒)dBB,

Proposition 3 lets us distinguish three subsets of self-approving systems, which differ in their
resident reputation equilibrium. The following lemma makes this partition explicit.

3.2

The following proposition provides simple necessary and sufficient conditions for a moral
system to be self-cooperative. Its proof uses Lemma 5.
Proposition 4 (Necessary and sufficient conditions for a conditional system to be self-cooperative).
A conditional system is self-cooperative if and only if either
(i) it is self-approving and pGG = C holds, or
(ii) it is self-disapproving and pBB = C holds.
Proof. By Lemma 5, we can write lim𝜒→0
 𝜅𝑝,𝑑

𝜖=0 = 𝑞𝑝(𝛾), where
𝛾= lim
𝜒→0

𝑔∗
𝑝,𝑑

𝜖=0
(79)
and where 𝑞𝑝is given by (76). Substituting all possible choices of (pBB, pBG, pGB, pGG) into 𝑞𝑝(𝛾)
yields the 16 cases listed in Table 3.
Since 𝑝is conditional, there are three subcases to consider.
1. Suppose that pBB = pGG = D holds. Then, 𝑞𝑝(𝛾) = (pBG + pGB) 𝛾(1−𝛾) with 1 ≤pBG+pGB ≤
2 (since 𝑝is conditional, pBG and pGB cannot both be D). Since 𝛾(1−𝛾) ≤1/4 for 𝛾∈[0, 1],
it follows that max0≤𝛾≤1 𝑞𝑝(𝛾) ≤1/2. Hence, 𝑝can never be self-cooperative under 𝑑.
2. Suppose that pGG = C holds. Then, 𝑞𝑝(1) = pGG = 1. Therefore, if (𝑝, 𝑑) is self-approving
(so that 𝛾= 1 by Definition 8 and Eq. (79)), then it is self-cooperative. This proves the
sufficient condition of part (i).
28


3. Suppose that pBB = C holds. Then, 𝑞𝑝(0) = pBB = 1. Hence, if (𝑝, 𝑑) is self-disapproving
(so that 𝛾= 0 by Definition 9 and Eq. (79)), then it is self-cooperative. This proves the
sufficient condition of part (ii).
Finally, we prove the necessity of conditions (i)–(ii). Suppose (𝑝, 𝑑) is self-cooperative, so that
𝑞𝑝(𝛾) = 1. If 0 < 𝛾< 1, all four weights (1−𝛾)2, 𝛾(1−𝛾), 𝛾(1−𝛾), and 𝛾2 in 𝑞𝑝(𝛾) are strictly
positive, so 𝑞𝑝(𝛾) = 1 would require pBB = pGG = C and pBG = pGB = C, that is, 𝑝= ALLC; since
𝑝is conditional, this is impossible, and hence 𝑞𝑝(𝛾) < 1, a contradiction. Therefore 𝛾∈{0, 1},
so (𝑝, 𝑑) is self-approving or self-disapproving. If 𝛾= 1, then pGG = 𝑞𝑝(1) = 1, so pGG = C and
(i) holds; if 𝛾= 0, then pBB = 𝑞𝑝(0) = 1, so pBB = C and (ii) holds. This concludes the proof.
□
Corollary 2 (Characterization of righteous systems). Let (𝑝, 𝑑) be self-approving. Then (𝑝, 𝑑)
is self-cooperative (and hence also righteous) if and only if pGG = C.
Proof. Being self-approving, (𝑝, 𝑑) is not self-disapproving, so condition (ii) of Proposition 4
cannot hold. Hence (𝑝, 𝑑) is self-cooperative if and only if condition (i) holds, that is, if and
only if pGG = C. Righteousness then follows from Definition 11, since (𝑝, 𝑑) is self-approving by
assumption.
□
Proposition 4 shows that the self-cooperative systems form two disjoint classes. The first is
the set of self-approving systems satisfying pGG = C; by Corollary 2, these coincide with the
righteous systems of Definition 11. The second is the set of self-disapproving systems satisfying
pBB = C.
Together with Proposition 3, Corollary 2 lets us simplify the characterization of the set R of
righteous systems given in Eq. (42) to
R = {(𝑝, 𝑑) |(𝑝, 𝑑) ∈C, pGG = C, dGG = G, and dBB + dBG + dGB ≥2} .
(80)
This characterization also lets us count the righteous systems, a figure reported in Fig. 4.
Proposition 5 (Cardinality of R). The set of righteous systems contains |R| = 114,688 moral
systems.
Proof. An assessment rule 𝑑is a function of 24 = 16 argument combinations, hence a string of 16
bits; a condition that leaves 𝑘of these bits unconstrained is satisfied by 2𝑘assessment rules. We
count the admissible systems by multiplying, over action rules and constrained bits, the number of
choices each leaves open. By Eq. (80) a righteous system is conditional and has pGG = C. Fixing
pGG = C leaves 23 = 8 strategies; one of them, the unconditional cooperator ALLC (𝑝= CCCC),
is excluded because a righteous system is conditional (Definition 11), leaving 7 admissible action
rules. Each moreover satisfies dGG = G together with dBB + dBG + dGB ≥2, which constrain four
distinct bits of 𝑑: dGG is fixed to G, while of the eight assignments of (dBB, dBG, dGB) only the four
with at least two G’s are admissible. The remaining 12 bits are free, so
|R| = 7 · 4 · 212 = 114,688.
□
3.3
Righteousness and reputational dominance of consistent systems
Every consistent system is self-approving, as the following proposition records.
29


Proposition 6 (Consistent systems are self-approving). If (𝑝, 𝑑) is consistent, then it lies in
AGGGG and is, in particular, self-approving. That is, Q ⊂AGGGG ⊂A.
Proof. By condition (43a) of Definition 13, a consistent system satisfies dGG = G and dBB = dBG =
dGB = G, so that dBB + dBG + dGB = 3 ≥2. By Proposition 3, it is therefore self-approving. Since,
in addition, all four assessment bits equal G, it lies in the subset AGGGG (item 3 of Lemma 4);
hence Q ⊂AGGGG ⊂A.
□
By Definition 14, a consistent discriminating system (𝑝, 𝑑) is one whose action rule 𝑝is
discriminating and consistent with 𝑑.
Proposition 7 (Consistent discriminating systems are righteous). If (𝑝, 𝑑) is consistent discrimi-
nating, then it is righteous. That is, QD ⊂R.
Proof. Since 𝑝is discriminating, it is conditional and satisfies pGG = C (Definitions 2 and 3).
Being consistent, (𝑝, 𝑑) is self-approving (Proposition 6); together with pGG = C, Proposition 4(i)
then makes it self-cooperative. A system that is both self-approving and self-cooperative is
righteous (Definition 11), so QD ⊂R.
□
The remainder of this subsection characterizes consistency in reputational terms. The
intuition is that a consistent assessment rule labels as good exactly those who follow the resident
prescription, so no alternative action rule should be able to match the resident’s reputation
under 𝑑. The next two propositions make this claim precise at 𝜖= 0: together, they show that a
system is consistent if and only if its resident action rule attains a strictly higher reputation than
every mutant action rule.
