Combining Propositions 8 and 9 yields the reputational characterization of consistency
anticipated at the start of this discussion.
Corollary 3 (Reputational characterization of consistency). Let 𝜖= 0 and 𝜒∈(0, 1/2). A system
(𝑝, 𝑑) is consistent if and only if ℎ∗

3.4
Righteousness of the leading eight
The leading eight (60) have action rule 𝑝= *CDC. Together with the shorthand (16d) and the
leading-eight assessment rule (48), this implies
dBG = dGB = dGG = G.
(87)
These values already place the leading eight among the righteous systems.
Proposition 10 (Leading-eight systems are righteous). Every leading-eight moral system is
righteous; that is, L ⊂R. Moreover, those in groups I and II belong to AGGGG, while those in
group III belong to ABGGG.
Proof. By Eq. (87), dGG = G and at least two of {dBB, dBG, dGB} equal G, so the leading eight are
self-approving (Proposition 3). Since pGG = C, they are also self-cooperative (Corollary 2), and
hence righteous (Definition 11); thus L ⊂R. For the finer placement, Eqs. (49)–(57) give dBB = G
for groups I and II and dBB = B for group III. Combined with Eq. (87), the former therefore have
all four assessment bits equal to G and lie in AGGGG, while the latter lie in ABGGG.
□
3.5
Universal stability within the set of righteous systems
Many moral systems of interest are righteous, notably the consistent discriminating systems
(Proposition 7) and the leading-eight systems (Proposition 10). It is therefore natural to ask
which righteous systems are universally stable (Definition 12). We show that a righteous system
is universally stable if and only if it is a contrite consistent discriminating system; that is, the
universally stable systems in the righteous set R (Eq. (80)) are exactly QCDISC (Eq. (45)).
We establish necessity by eliminating systems one class of invader at a time, producing
a strictly decreasing chain R ⊃R′ ⊃R′′ ⊃R′′′ = QCDISC: we discard in turn the systems
invaded by ALLD (leaving R′; Section 3.5.2), by ALLC (leaving R′′; Section 3.5.3), and by
some discriminating strategy (leaving R′′′ = QCDISC; Section 3.5.4). Sufficiency then follows by
verifying that every system in QCDISC resists invasion by all alternative strategies (Section 3.5.5).
Fig. 4 maps this chain and locates the consistent discriminating systems and the leading eight
within it.

3.5.2
Invasion attempts by ALLD
For the mutant ˜𝑝= ALLD,
˜pBB = ˜pBG = ˜pGB = ˜pGG = D.
(98)
We seek the subset R′ ⊆R of righteous systems that resist such invasion across cooperative
dilemmas. This subset is characterized by the following two propositions, according to whether
dGB = G or dGB = B; both require pGB = D and differ only in condition (ii). We prove them below
by a single argument.
Proposition 11 (Stability against ALLD for righteous systems with dGB = G). Let 𝜖= 0 and
𝜒→0. A righteous system (𝑝, 𝑑) with dGB = G resists invasion attempts by ALLD across
cooperative dilemmas if and only if
(i) pGB = D, and
(ii) dBGDD = dGGDC = B.
Proposition 12 (Stability against ALLD for righteous systems with dGB = B). Let 𝜖= 0 and
𝜒→0. A righteous system (𝑝, 𝑑) with dGB = B resists invasion attempts by ALLD across
cooperative dilemmas if and only if
(i) pGB = D, and
(ii) dBGDD = B and either dGGDC = B or 𝑑(B, B, D, pBB) = B.

(103)
which follows from Eqs. (101) and (98).
1. Suppose that (𝑝, 𝑑) ∈ABGGG ∪AGGGG. Inspection of Table 4 shows that condition (102)
holds if and only if
˜dBG = 𝑑(B, G, ˜pBG, pGB) = dBGDD = B
(104)
and
˜dGG = 𝑑(G, G, ˜pGG, pGG) = dGGDC = B
(105)
both hold.
2. Suppose that (𝑝, 𝑑) ∈AG**G, so that dBB = dGG = G and dBG ≠dGB (Eq. (70)). By the
identity (103), ˜dGB = dGB, giving two subcases.
(a) If (dBG, dGB) = (B, G), then ˜dGB = dGB = G, and Table 4 shows that condition (102)
holds if and only if both (104) and (105) hold — the same condition as in case (i).
(b) If (dBG, dGB) = (G, B), then ˜dGB = dGB = B, and Table 4 shows that condition (102)
holds if and only if (104) holds, together with either (105) or
˜dBB = 𝑑(B, B, ˜pBB, pBB) = 𝑑(B, B, D, pBB) = B.
Together with the necessary condition pGB = D (101), these cases give condition (ii) of Propo-
sition 11 when dGB = G (case (i) and subcase (ii-a)) and of Proposition 12 when dGB = B
(subcase (ii-b)).
□
These propositions have a further consequence for righteous systems with dBG = G, which we
use repeatedly in the analyses that follow.
Corollary 4 (Stability against ALLD forces pBG = C when dBG = G). Let 𝜖= 0 and 𝜒→0. If
a righteous system (𝑝, 𝑑) with dBG = G resists invasion attempts by ALLD across cooperative
dilemmas, then pBG = C.
38


Proof. Suppose instead that pBG = D. Resistance to ALLD forces pGB = D (101), so that
dBG = 𝑑(B, G, pBG, pGB) = 𝑑(B, G, D, D) = dBGDD; with dBG = G this gives dBGDD = G, contradicting
the condition dBGDD = B that resistance requires (Propositions 11 and 12).
□
We denote by R′ the subset of righteous systems that resist invasion by ALLD across
cooperative dilemmas, as characterized by Propositions 11 and 12. Combining these two
propositions with Eq. (80):
R′ = {(𝑝, 𝑑) ∈R | pGB = D, dGB = G, dBGDD = B, dGGDC = B}
∪{(𝑝, 𝑑) ∈R | pGB = D, dGB = B, dBGDD = B,

dGGDC = B or 𝑑(B, B, D, pBB) = B
	
.
(106)
Since systems in R′ satisfy both pGG = C and pGB = D, every (𝑝, 𝑑) ∈R′ has a discriminating
action rule. This characterization lets us immediately locate two families of systems of special
interest within the subset R′. First, we can verify that all leading-eight systems satisfy the
conditions defining R′. Second, among the four subsets of consistent discriminating systems, two
belong to R′ whereas the other two are excluded already at this stage.
Proposition 13 (Leading-eight systems belong to R′). L ⊂R′.
Proof. All leading-eight action rules are discriminating, so pGB = D, and their assessment rules
are third-order with dBGD∗= B, dGGD∗= B, and dGBD∗= G (Eqs. (49)–(57)). Since the norm ignores
the co-player’s action, dBGDD = B, dGGDC = B, and dGB = 𝑑(G, B, D, pBG) = dGBD∗= G. Together with
L ⊂R (Proposition 10), these satisfy the dGB = G branch of Eq. (106).
□
Proposition 14 (Consistent discriminating systems and R′). QCDISC ∪QDISC ⊂R′, while
QPDISC ∩R′ = QODISC ∩R′ = ∅.
Proof. For QCDISC and QDISC: both are discriminating with pBG = C, so that dGB = 𝑑(G, B, D, C) =
dGBDC; Eqs. (45) and (44) give dBGDD = B, dGGDC = B, and dGBDC = G, whence dGB = G and the
dGB = G branch of Eq. (106) is satisfied. For QPDISC and QODISC: Eqs. (47) and (46) include
dBGDD = G, which violates the necessary condition dBGDD = B for R′ membership.
□
The characterization (106) also determines the cardinality of R′.
Proposition 15 (Cardinality of R′). The set R′ contains |R′| = 12,800 moral systems.
Proof. We count the admissible systems as in the proof of Proposition 5, recalling that a condition
leaving 𝑘bits of 𝑑unconstrained is satisfied by 2𝑘assessment rules. By Eq. (106) every system in
R′ has pGG = C and pGB = D, leaving four action rules indexed by (pBB, pBG) ∈{C, D}2; Eq. (106)
is moreover the disjoint union of a branch with dGB = G and one with dGB = B. For each action
rule and branch, the defining conditions fix or restrict a few bits of 𝑑and leave the rest free.
For instance, in the branch dGB = G with pBG = C, the bits dGB = 𝑑(G, B, D, C), dBG = 𝑑(B, G, C, D)
and dBGDD = 𝑑(B, G, D, D) are distinct; the conditions dGG = G, dGGDC = B, dGB = G, dBGDD = B
fix four bits, and dBB + dBG + dGB ≥2 (with dGB = G) admits three of the four assignments of
(dBB, dBG), giving 3 · 210 = 3,072. Coincidences among the constrained bits occur when pBG = D
(then dBG = dBGDD) or pBB = D (then dBB = 𝑑(B, B, D, pBB)) and lower the counts; in particular, the
dGB = B branch is empty when pBG = D, since then dBG = dBGDD = B and dGB = B would violate
dBB + dBG + dGB ≥2. Table 5 collects all cases; they sum to |R′| = 12,800.
□

3.5.3
Invasion attempts by ALLC
For the mutant ˜𝑝= ALLC,

We seek which systems in R′ also resist such invasion across cooperative dilemmas. These are
characterized by the following three propositions: the first shows that resistance requires dGB = G;
the other two then characterize the systems with dGB = G, according to whether dBG = B or
dBG = G. We prove all three together below.
Proposition 16 (Stability against ALLC requires dGB = G). Let 𝜖= 0 and 𝜒→0. If a righteous
system (𝑝, 𝑑) that resists invasion attempts by ALLD also resists invasion attempts by ALLC
across cooperative dilemmas, then dGB = G.
Proposition 16 implies that (𝑝, 𝑑) ∈R ∩AGGBG cannot be universally stable.
Proposition 17 (Stability against ALLC for righteous systems with dGB = G and dBG = B). Let
𝜖= 0 and 𝜒→0. A righteous system (𝑝, 𝑑) with dGB = G and dBG = B that resists invasion
attempts by ALLD also resists invasion attempts by ALLC across cooperative dilemmas if and
only if
(i) dBGCD = B, and
(ii) either 𝑑(G, B, C, pBG) = B or 𝑑(B, B, C, pBB) = B.
Proposition 18 (Stability against ALLC for righteous systems with dGB = G and dBG = G). Let
𝜖= 0 and 𝜒→0. A righteous system (𝑝, 𝑑) with dGB = G and dBG = G that resists invasion
attempts by ALLD also resists invasion attempts by ALLC across cooperative dilemmas if and
only if dGBCC = B.

˜dBG = 𝑑(B, G, ˜pBG, pGB) = dBGCD = B

As for R′, we locate the leading-eight and consistent discriminating systems within R′′.

Proposition 19 (Leading-eight systems and R′′). A leading-eight system belongs to R′′ if and
only if dGBC∗= B. The systems satisfying this condition are {L2, L5, L6, L8}; the remaining four
systems {L1, L3, L4, L7} belong to R′ \ R′′.
Proof. By Proposition 13, all leading-eight systems belong to R′ with dGB = G. Since all have
dBGC∗= G (Eqs. (49)–(57)), it follows that dBG = dBGCD = G, so every leading-eight system falls
into the second branch of Eq. (114), which requires dGBCC = B. Since the norm ignores the
co-player’s action, dGBCC = dGBC∗, so the condition dGBCC = B is just dGBC∗= B. Inspecting the
definitions: L2, L5, L6, and L8 have dGBC∗= B; L1, L3, L4, and L7 have dGBC∗= G.
□
Proposition 20 (Consistent discriminating systems and R′′). QCDISC ∪QDISC ⊂R′′, while
QPDISC ∩R′′ = QODISC ∩R′′ = ∅.
Proof. Since QPDISC ∩R′ = QODISC ∩R′ = ∅(Proposition 14) and R′′ ⊂R′, the same
holds for R′′. For QCDISC and QDISC: both have pBG = C, so dBG = 𝑑(B, G, C, D) = dBGCD and
dGB = 𝑑(G, B, D, C) = dGBDC; Eqs. (45) and (44) give dBGCD = G, dGBDC = G, and dGBCC = B, whence
dBG = dGB = G and the second branch of Eq. (114) is satisfied.

Proposition 21 (Cardinality of R′′). The set R′′ contains |R′′| = 4,608 moral systems.
Proof. We count the admissible systems as before, recalling that a condition leaving 𝑘bits
of 𝑑unconstrained is satisfied by 2𝑘assessment rules. Both branches of Eq. (114) require
dGB = G, so R′′ lies within the dGB = G branch of R′; on top of the conditions imposed there it
adds constraints that split on dBG. For instance, with pBG = C and dBG = G: since pBG = C we
have dBG = 𝑑(B, G, C, D), so dBG = G and dGBCC = B fix two further bits, the majority condition
dBB + dBG + dGB ≥2 is then automatic and dBB is free; six bits are fixed and ten free, giving
210 = 1,024. The dBG = G branch is moreover empty when pBG = D, since then dBG = dBGDD, which
R′ already forces to B. Table 8 collects all cases; they sum to |R′′| = 4,608.
□
A direct enumeration of all 14 × 216 conditional systems confirms the cardinalities |R|, |R′|,
and |R′′| reported in Propositions 5–21 and in Fig. 4.
3.5.4
Invasion attempts by a discriminating mutant
We now determine which systems in R′′ also resist invasion by a discriminating mutant ˜𝑝≠𝑝
across cooperative dilemmas. Such a mutant satisfies
˜pGG = C
and
˜pGB = D.
(115)
The main results are the following two propositions, which we prove in turn below.
Proposition 22 (Invasion by discriminating mutants for righteous systems with dBG = B or
dBB = B). Let 𝜖= 0 and 𝜒→0. A righteous system (𝑝, 𝑑) with dBG = B or dBB = B that resists
invasion attempts by ALLD and ALLC cannot resist invasion attempts by at least one alternative
discriminating strategy.
Propositions 16 and 22 together imply that (𝑝, 𝑑) ∈R ∩(AGGBG ∪AGBGG ∪ABGGG) cannot be
universally stable; among the righteous classes, this leaves only AGGGG, which the next proposition
characterizes.
Proposition 23 (Stability against discriminating mutants for righteous systems in AGGGG). Let
𝜖= 0 and 𝜒→0. A righteous system (𝑝, 𝑑) with dBB = dBG = dGB = dGG = G that resists invasion
attempts by ALLD and ALLC also resists invasion attempts by ˜𝑝∈{DISC, CDISC} with ˜𝑝≠𝑝
across cooperative dilemmas if and only if pBB = C and dBBDC = B.

Preliminary observations.
Since both 𝑝and ˜𝑝are discriminating, the following equalities
hold:
( ˜𝜔GG)𝜖=0 = (𝜔GG)𝜖=0 = 𝑅,
(116a)
( ˜𝜔GB)𝜖=0 = (𝜔GB)𝜖=0 = 𝑣(pGB, pBG) = 𝑣(D, pBG).
(116b)
Moreover, ˜pGG = pGG and ˜pGB = pGB, so from dGG = G (97a) and dGB = G (Proposition 16) we
obtain
˜dGG = 𝑑(G, G, ˜pGG, pGG) = 𝑑(G, G, pGG, pGG) = dGG = G
(117)
and
˜dGB = 𝑑(G, B, ˜pGB, pBG) = 𝑑(G, B, pGB, pBG) = dGB = G.
(118)
These two equalities, ˜dGG = ˜dGB = G, hold throughout the analysis below. The mutant assessment
bits ˜dBG and ˜dBB depend on which class of systems is considered, and are determined case by case.
Proof of Proposition 22. We show that for each of the classes AGBGG and ABGGG there exists a
discriminating mutant that invades for some cooperative dilemma.
Case AGBGG (i.e., dBG = B). The Puiseux-series expansion of 𝑤(𝜒) (93) is
𝑤(𝜒) = (𝜔GG)𝜖=0 + [(𝜔BG)𝜖=0 + (𝜔GB)𝜖=0 −2 (𝜔GG)𝜖=0] √𝜒+ 𝑂(𝜒).
(119)
By Propositions 11 and 17, dBGDD = dBGCD = B holds. Hence, regardless of ˜pBG,
˜dBG = 𝑑(B, G, ˜pBG, pGB) = B.
(120)
Consider the discriminating mutant ˜𝑝with ˜pBG ≠pBG and ˜pBB = pBB. Then ˜dBB = dBB = G, and
the mutant assessment bits are (˜dBB, ˜dBG, ˜dGB, ˜dGG) = (G, B, G, G), so ˜𝑤(𝜒) has the same expansion
form as 𝑤(𝜒). The leading term of Δ𝑤(𝜒) reduces to
(𝜔BG)𝜖=0 −( ˜𝜔BG)𝜖=0 ,
(121)
which equals 𝑃−𝑆if pBG = D (negative for snowdrift games) or 𝑆−𝑃if pBG = C (negative for
prisoner’s dilemmas and stag hunts), by Eq. (108a). Hence (𝑝, 𝑑) ∈AGBGG cannot be universally
stable.
Case ABGGG (i.e., dBG = G, dBB = B). Since dBG = G, Corollary 4 gives pBG = C. Consider a
discriminating mutant ˜𝑝with ˜pBG = pBG but ˜pBB ≠pBB. Then
˜dBG = 𝑑(B, G, pBG, pGB) = dBG = G
(122)
and
( ˜𝜔BG)𝜖=0 = (𝜔BG)𝜖=0 = vCD = 𝑆.
(123)
The second-order expansion of 𝑤(𝜒) is
𝑤(𝜒) = (𝜔GG)𝜖=0 + [(𝜔BG)𝜖=0 + (𝜔GB)𝜖=0 −2 (𝜔GG)𝜖=0] 𝜒
+ [(𝜔BB)𝜖=0 −(𝜔GG)𝜖=0] 𝜒2 + 𝑂(𝜒3).
(124)
Since ˜dGG = ˜dGB = ˜dBG = G, the mutant assessment bits are (˜dBB, G, G, G) with ˜dBB ∈{G, B}.
Computing ˜𝑤(𝜒) from (94) and subtracting, the leading term of Δ𝑤(𝜒) at order 𝜒2 is reported
in Table 9 for each subcase.
When ˜dBB = G, the leading term evaluates to 𝑆−𝑇< 0 (if pBB = C) or 𝑃−𝑅< 0 (if pBB = D),
both always negative. When ˜dBB = B, it evaluates to 𝑅−𝑇(if pBB = C), which is negative for

games. In every case, the mutant invades for at least one class of cooperative dilemmas. Hence
(𝑝, 𝑑) ∈ABGGG cannot be universally stable.

subtracting, we obtain the leading terms of Δ𝑤(𝜒) reported in Table 10.
When ˜dBB = G, the leading term (𝜔BB)𝜖=0 −( ˜𝜔BB)𝜖=0 evaluates to 𝑅−𝑇for pBB = C (so
˜pBB = D), negative for prisoner’s dilemmas and snowdrift games, or to 𝑃−𝑆for pBB = D (so
˜pBB = C), negative for snowdrift games. In either case the mutant invades for at least one class, so
˜dBB = B is necessary for universal stability.
When ˜dBB = B, the leading term equals (𝜔BB)𝜖=0 −(𝜔BG)𝜖=0 + (𝜔GG)𝜖=0 −( ˜𝜔BB)𝜖=0.
• If pBB = D (i.e., 𝑝= DISC, ˜𝑝= CDISC), this reduces to 𝑅+ 𝑃−2𝑆. While positive for
prisoner’s dilemmas and stag hunts, this quantity is negative for some snowdrift games, so
the resident can be invaded.
• If pBB = C (i.e., 𝑝= CDISC, ˜𝑝= DISC), this reduces to 2𝑅−𝑇−𝑆, which is positive
across all cooperative dilemmas by condition (6). Hence the resident resists invasion.
We conclude that universal stability requires (𝑝, 𝑑) ∈AGGGG, pBB = C, and also ˜dBB =
𝑑(B, B, ˜pBB, pBB) = dBBDC = B.
□
We denote by R′′′ the subset of R′′ that resists invasion by any discriminating strategy
across cooperative dilemmas, as characterized by Propositions 22 and 23. These, together with
Propositions 11, 16, 18 and Corollary 4, imply that the systems in R′′′ satisfy 𝑝= CCDC together
with dBBCC = dBGCD = dGBDC = dGGCC = G and dBBDC = dBGDD = dGBCC = dGGDC = B; by Eq. (45), this
set of righteous systems coincides with the contrite consistent discriminating systems, i.e.,
R′′′ = QCDISC.
(126)
46


Since every discriminating action rule admits 28 = 256 consistent assessment rules (Section 2.3),
it follows in particular that |R′′′| = |QCDISC| = 256, the last of the cardinalities reported in Fig. 4.
As for R′ and R′′, we locate the leading-eight and consistent discriminating systems within
R′′′. Only one leading-eight system survives, and among the consistent discriminating families
only QCDISC meets R′′′ — in fact equal to it.
Proposition 24 (Leading-eight systems and R′′′). Among the leading eight, only L2 belongs to
R′′′; the other seven (L1, L3, L4, L5, L6, L7, L8) do not.
Proof. Since R′′′ = QCDISC (126), membership in R′′′ requires 𝑝= CCDC. Among the leading
eight, only L1 and L2 use 𝑝= CCDC; all others use 𝑝= DCDC and are thus excluded. L1 fails
because dGBC∗= G (i.e., dGBCC = G ≠B), violating the condition dGBCC = B in Eq. (45). L2 satisfies
all conditions of QCDISC (cf. Eqs. (51) and (45)).
□
Proposition 25 (Consistent discriminating systems and R′′′). QCDISC = R′′′, while QDISC∩R′′′ =
QPDISC ∩R′′′ = QODISC ∩R′′′ = ∅.
Proof. The equality QCDISC = R′′′ is Eq. (126). Since QDISC, QPDISC, and QODISC use the action
rules DCDC, DDDC, and CDDC respectively — all different from CCDC — each is disjoint from
QCDISC = R′′′.
□
3.5.5
The universal stability theorem
We are now in a position to prove the main result of this section.
Theorem 1 (Universal stability within righteous systems). Let 𝜖= 0 and 𝜒→0. A righteous
system is universally stable if and only if it is a contrite consistent discriminating system.
Proof. Necessity. A universally stable righteous system resists ALLD, ALLC, and every discrim-
inating strategy; by the successive eliminations of Sections 3.5.2, 3.5.3, and 3.5.4, it therefore
lies in R′′′ = QCDISC (126), i.e., it is contrite consistent discriminating.
Sufficiency. Conversely, let (𝑝, 𝑑) ∈QCDISC, so that 𝑝= CDISC. We compute Δ𝑤(𝜒) for each
of the 15 mutant strategies ˜𝑝≠𝑝; since all systems in QCDISC share the same reputation equilibria
and payoffs at 𝜖= 0 (Remark 1), Δ𝑤(𝜒) does not depend on which consistent assessment rule
accompanies CDISC, and the results, collected in Table 11, hold for all of QCDISC at once.
A direct inspection of Table 11 reveals three conditions for (𝑝, 𝑑) ∈QCDISC to resist invasion
by a mutant strategy ˜𝑝≠𝑝:
1. If ˜𝑝= *D**, then CDISC resists invasion attempts if and only if 𝑅> 𝑃, which always
holds across cooperative dilemmas (3).
2. If ˜𝑝= *CCC, then CDISC resists invasion attempts if and only if 𝑇> 𝑆, which always
holds across cooperative dilemmas (12).
3. Otherwise, CDISC resists invasion attempts if and only if 2𝑅> 𝑇+ 𝑆, which always holds
across cooperative dilemmas (6).
As all three conditions hold across cooperative dilemmas, (𝑝, 𝑑) resists every mutant strategy
and is therefore universally stable.
□

3.6
Stability analysis of consistent discriminating systems
Consistent discriminating systems occupy a central role in our analysis: they are righteous
(Proposition 7), and they form the dense cluster in the upper-right corner of the stability–
cooperation scatter plot shown in Fig. 2a of the main text. Section 3.5 establishes that, among
righteous systems, universal stability — resistance to invasion across the full space of cooperative
dilemmas — is exclusive to consistent discriminating systems with CDISC as action rule
(Theorem 1).
We now ask how consistent discriminating systems with the three other action rules (DISC,
ODISC, and PDISC) fare. Universal stability being out of reach for these systems, we determine
instead the limiting values of their per-class stability indices 𝜎PD, 𝜎SG, and 𝜎SH, thereby
identifying the classes of cooperative dilemma (prisoner’s dilemma, snowdrift game, stag hunt)
in which each system remains stable. We proceed as in the previous section, expanding the
payoff difference Δ𝑤(𝜒) around 𝜒= 0 for every alternative mutant strategy; since all 256
consistent systems sharing an action rule have identical reputation equilibria and payoffs at 𝜖= 0
(Remark 1), these expansions depend only on the action rule, and each result below holds for
every consistent assessment rule at once. For ease of comparison, we first restate the result for
CDISC (Corollary 5) and then treat each remaining case in turn; Table 15 collects the results.
3.6.1

Since systems in QCDISC are righteous (Proposition 7) and are precisely the contrite consistent
discriminating systems (Definition 7), Theorem 1 implies that they are universally stable, and
Definition 12 immediately gives:

3.6.2
Consistent discriminating systems with DISC as action rule
It is clear from the characterizations of the sets QDISC (44) and R′′ (114) that QDISC ⊂R′′ holds,
so that a moral system (𝑝, 𝑑) ∈QDISC resists invasion attempts by ALLD and ALLC across
cooperative dilemmas. It is equally clear, from the analysis in Section 3.5.4, that such a system can
be invaded by CDISC for some games in our payoff space. The following proposition completes
the analysis by considering all alternative mutant strategies and quantifies the resulting stability
indices.
Proposition 26 (Stability of consistent discriminating systems with DISC as action rule). For
𝜖= 0 and in the limit 𝜒→0, a consistent discriminating system with DISC as action rule
is stable across prisoner’s dilemmas and stag hunts, but it can be invaded by CDISC if the
cooperative dilemma is a snowdrift game for which 𝑅+ 𝑃< 2𝑆holds. Thus:

by a mutant strategy ˜𝑝≠𝑝:
49


1. If ˜𝑝= *D**, then DISC resists invasion attempts if and only if 𝑅> 𝑃, which always holds
across cooperative dilemmas (3).
2. If ˜𝑝= *CCC, then DISC resists invasion attempts if and only if 𝑇> 𝑆, which always holds
across cooperative dilemmas (12).
3. If ˜𝑝= CCDC (i.e., CDISC), then DISC resists invasion attempts if and only if 𝑅+ 𝑃> 2𝑆,
which holds for all prisoner’s dilemmas (9) and stag hunts (11), but not for all snowdrift
games (10).
4. For all remaining mutant strategies, DISC resists invasion attempts if and only if 2𝑅> 𝑇+𝑆,
which always holds across cooperative dilemmas (6).
The conditions in items 1, 2, and 4 hold across all cooperative dilemmas, and the condition in
item 3 holds across all prisoner’s dilemmas and stag hunts; the stability indices for these two
classes thus converge to one. Within snowdrift games, the only binding condition is 𝑅+ 𝑃> 2𝑆:
it fails precisely for the games with 𝑅+ 𝑃< 2𝑆, in which CDISC invades, and by Lemma 1 the
snowdrift games satisfying 𝑅+ 𝑃> 2𝑆make up a fraction 2/3 of the class.
□

3.6.4
Consistent discriminating systems with PDISC as action rule
It is clear from the characterizations of the sets QPDISC (47) and R′ (106) that QPDISC ⊄R′ holds,
so that a moral system (𝑝, 𝑑) ∈QPDISC can be invaded by ALLD for some cooperative dilemmas.
The following proposition completes the analysis by considering all alternative mutant strategies
and quantifies the resulting stability indices.

if the cooperative dilemma is a prisoner’s dilemma for which 2𝑅< 𝑇+ 𝑃holds. Thus:

3.6.5
Summary: consistent discriminating systems
Table 15 collects the stability results for the four discriminating action rules under a consistent
assessment rule (Corollary 5 and Propositions 26, 27, and 28). The results confirm that, among
consistent discriminating systems, universal stability is exclusive to those with CDISC as action
rule: every other action rule fails in at least one class of cooperative dilemma. Systems with
PDISC or ODISC as action rule can be invaded in the strict subset of prisoner’s dilemmas for
which 2𝑅< 𝑇+ 𝑃holds, while systems with DISC as action rule can be invaded by CDISC in
the strict subset of snowdrift games for which 𝑅+ 𝑃< 2𝑆holds.
3.7
Stability analysis of the leading eight
We now determine the limiting per-class stability indices of the leading-eight moral systems
(Section 2.4). As we shall see, L2 is the only leading-eight system that achieves universal stability;
every other member fails in at least one class of cooperative dilemma.
By Propositions 19 and 24, the leading eight are distributed across the hierarchy R ⊃R′ ⊃
R′′ ⊃R′′′ of Section 3.5 as follows: L2 belongs to R′′′; L5, L6, and L8 belong to R′′ \ R′′′; and
L1, L3, L4, and L7 belong to R′ \ R′′. This placement organizes the analysis into three cases:

3.7.2
L2 (CDISC, Consistent Standing)
L2 belongs to QCDISC (cf. Eqs. (51) and (45)), the set of contrite consistent discriminating
systems. In contrast with L1, its assessment rule judges cooperation with bad co-players as
bad (dGBC∗= B), so a *CCC mutant loses its good standing whenever it cooperates with a bad
co-player, and is subsequently exploited by discriminating residents. As a result, the binding
stability condition against these mutants is 𝑇> 𝑆(Table 11) rather than L1’s 𝑇> 𝑅(Table 16);
since 𝑇> 𝑆holds across all cooperative dilemmas (12), L2 resists these mutants even in stag
hunts, where L1 is invaded.

3.7.4
L4 (DISC, SS-SJ)
L4 belongs to R′ \ R′′ (Proposition 19); its assessment rule has dGBC∗= G and dBBC∗= B. The

stability indices.

is a stag hunt. Thus:

All four conditions hold across prisoner’s dilemmas, so the corresponding stability index converges

in which the mutants of item 2 invade; the stability index for this class thus converges to zero.

despite having dBBC∗= B like L4 and L6. The following proposition quantifies the resulting

3.7.9
Summary: the leading eight
Table 22 collects the stability results for the eight leading-eight moral systems (Propositions 29–
36). L2 (CDISC, Consistent Standing) is the only universally stable system; every other member
can be invaded in at least one class of cooperative dilemma. All six systems with DISC as
action rule can be invaded by CDISC in snowdrift games — in every such game for L3, L5,
L7, and L8, and in those satisfying 𝑅+ 𝑃< 2𝑆for L4 and L6. The reason is that 𝑆> 𝑃holds
in snowdrift games: when meeting a co-player in bad standing while in bad standing itself, a
contrite discriminator cooperates (earning 𝑆) where the resident defects (earning 𝑃), and thus
profits from the deviation. The four systems with dGBC∗= G (L1, L3, L4, and L7) — among them
L1, which pairs the CDISC action rule with the inconsistent Standing norm — fail in every stag
hunt, where 𝑅> 𝑇holds: mutants that also cooperate with co-players in bad standing retain their
good standing and earn 𝑅where residents earn only 𝑇. These results reinforce the conclusion of
the preceding sections: among the leading eight, universal stability requires both consistency and
contrition, a combination found only in L2.
4
Numerical results
The analytical results of the preceding sections were derived in the idealized regime of zero
execution errors (𝜖= 0) and vanishing assignment errors (𝜒→0), and the numerical results of
the main text were computed for a single combination of small error rates (𝜖= 𝜒= 0.02). This
section presents numerical results verifying that our findings are robust to these choices. We first
count moral systems up to the mirror symmetry of Section 2.5 and specify the representatives our
numerical explorations evaluate (Section 4.1), and then detail the computational pipeline behind
61


these numerical results (Section 4.2). We finally examine how the stability and cooperation
indices behave across a grid of error-rate combinations (Section 4.3), how stability is distributed
across the three classes of cooperative dilemma (Section 4.4), and how results change under the
alternative, area-weighted construction of the average stability index (Section 4.5).
4.1
Mirror classes and representatives
Section 2.5 established that a moral system and its mirror are behaviorally equivalent: they share
the same cooperation and stability indices (Proposition 2), whereas label-dependent properties
such as self-approval hold for at most one member of each mirror pair (Corollary 1). Here we
extend these results to consistency, count moral systems up to the mirror symmetry, and specify
the representatives evaluated in our numerical explorations.
Consistency is a label-dependent property: it requires conformity with the resident prescription
to be rewarded with the particular label G. Unsurprisingly, it is not mirror-invariant.
Proposition 37 (Mirrors of consistent systems). Let (𝑝, 𝑑) be a consistent system. Then its mirror
( ˆ𝑝, ˆ𝑑) satisfies
ˆ𝑑(𝑟1, 𝑟2, ˆ𝑝(𝑟1, 𝑟2), ˆ𝑝(𝑟2, 𝑟1)) = B, and
(128a)
ˆ𝑑(𝑟1, 𝑟2, ¬ ˆ𝑝(𝑟1, 𝑟2), ˆ𝑝(𝑟2, 𝑟1)) = G
(128b)
for all 𝑟1, 𝑟2 ∈{B, G}. Consequently, the mirror of a consistent system is not consistent, no
consistent system is a self-mirror, and each mirror class contains at most one consistent system.
Proof. By (61), ˆ𝑑(𝑟1, 𝑟2, ˆ𝑝(𝑟1, 𝑟2), ˆ𝑝(𝑟2, 𝑟1)) = ¬ 𝑑(¬𝑟1, ¬𝑟2, 𝑝(¬𝑟1, ¬𝑟2), 𝑝(¬𝑟2, ¬𝑟1)) = ¬ G =
B, using the consistency condition (43a) at the reputation profile (¬𝑟1, ¬𝑟2); this establishes
Eq. (128a), and Eq. (128b) follows in the same way from (43b). Since consistency (43a) requires
the left-hand side of Eq. (128a) to equal G for all reputation profiles, ( ˆ𝑝, ˆ𝑑) is not consistent.
If a consistent system were a self-mirror, its mirror – the system itself – would be consistent,
contradicting the non-consistency of the mirror just established; the same argument shows that
the two members of a mirror pair cannot both be consistent.
□
In words, Eq. (128) says that the mirror of a consistent system is consistent with the labels
swapped: it rewards conformity with the label B, which plays for the mirror system the role that G
plays for the original one. This is precisely the sense in which the two systems are relabelings of
one another.
We now count self-mirrors, and with them the mirror classes.
Proposition 38 (Number of self-mirrors). A moral system (𝑝, 𝑑) is a self-mirror if and only if
both
pBB = pGG and pBG = pGB, and
(129a)
d𝑟1𝑟2𝑎1𝑎2 = ¬ d¬𝑟1 ¬𝑟2 𝑎1𝑎2 for all 𝑟1, 𝑟2, 𝑎1, 𝑎2
(129b)
hold. Exactly four strategies satisfy condition (129a) (the palindromic bitstrings DDDD, DCCD,
CDDC, and CCCC) and exactly 28 = 256 assessment rules satisfy condition (129b), so there are
4 × 256 = 1,024 self-mirrors.
62


Proof. Conditions (129a) and (129b) restate ˆ𝑝= 𝑝and ˆ𝑑= 𝑑, respectively, using (61). For
strategies, ˆ𝑝= 𝑝says that the bitstring pBBpBGpGBpGG is a palindrome, which leaves the two bits
pBB and pBG free: the four solutions are DDDD (ALLD), DCCD, CDDC (ODISC), and CCCC (ALLC).
For assessment rules, the map (𝑟1, 𝑟2, 𝑎1, 𝑎2) ↦→(¬𝑟1, ¬𝑟2, 𝑎1, 𝑎2) pairs the sixteen argument
profiles into eight pairs (it has no fixed point, since (𝑟1, 𝑟2) ≠(¬𝑟1, ¬𝑟2) always holds), and
on each pair the constraint in (129b) admits exactly two of the four joint assignments; hence
28 = 256 solutions.
□
There are exactly as many self-mirrors as consistent discriminating systems – 1,024 in
both cases – but the coincidence is accidental: the former number arises as (four palindromic
strategies) × (256 self-mirror assessment rules), the latter as (four discriminating strategies) ×
(256 consistent assessment rules per strategy), and the two sets are disjoint by Proposition 37. The
two sets even share a strategy: ODISC (𝑝= CDDC) is the unique discriminating strategy equal to
its own mirror – yet, again by Proposition 37, no consistent ODISC system is a self-mirror.
Proposition 39 (Number of mirror classes). Let M be a set of moral systems that is closed under
the mirror map, and let 𝑚be the number of self-mirrors in M. Then M partitions into
|M| −𝑚
2
+ 𝑚= |M| + 𝑚
2
(130)
mirror classes. In particular, the full space of 220 = 1,048,576 moral systems, which contains
𝑚= 1,024 self-mirrors (Proposition 38), partitions into  220 + 1,024 /2 = 524,800 mirror
classes.
Proof. The mirror map restricts to an involution on M, so it partitions M into classes of size
one or two, and the classes of size one are exactly the self-mirrors in M. The number of classes
is therefore 𝑚plus half the number |M| −𝑚of remaining systems.
□
Equation (130) is an instance of Burnside’s lemma (ref. 5, Chapter 10), applied to the
two-element group generated by the mirror map: the number of orbits (the mirror classes) equals
the average number of elements of M fixed by the group elements – |M| for the identity and 𝑚
for the mirror.
Beyond the full space, Eq. (130) applies to any mirror-closed subset, such as those obtained
by pairing the 24 strategies with a type class TU of assessment rules (Section 1.5): the mirror of a
rule of type U is again of type U, since the mirror negates reputation arguments and outputs but
never adds a dependence on a new argument. If U contains at least one reputation argument,
the negation (𝑟1, 𝑟2) ↦→(¬𝑟1, ¬𝑟2) pairs the 2|U| argument profiles of type U into 2|U|−1 pairs,
so that, by the argument in the proof of Proposition 38, exactly 22|U|−1 rules of type U are their
own mirrors; if U contains no reputation argument, a self-mirror rule would have to equal its
own negation, so there is none. For instance, the space explored by Ohtsuki and Iwasa2 pairs
the 24 strategies with the 223 = 256 third-order rules of type 𝑎1𝑟1𝑟2: it contains 4 × 222 = 26
self-mirrors (four palindromic strategies times 222 = 16 self-mirror rules), so Eq. (130) yields
 212 + 26 /2 = 2,080 mirror classes, recovering the count of ‘effective pairs’ derived in their
Appendix A.
Because both members of a mirror pair share the same cooperation and stability indices
(Proposition 2), numerical explorations of the space of moral systems need to evaluate only one
member per mirror class – a reduction already exploited by Ohtsuki and Iwasa2, who report a
single member of each pair and omit its mirror image. Sweeping the full space of moral systems,
however, requires an explicit selection rule, which the following definition provides.
63


Definition 16 (Majority-good representatives). Fix error rates 𝜒∈(0, 1/2) and 𝜖∈[0, 1/2).
The representative of a mirror class is: its unique member, if the class is a self-mirror; otherwise,
the member with 𝑔∗
𝑝,𝑑> 1/2, if there is one; otherwise (that is, if both members of the pair satisfy
𝑔∗
𝑝,𝑑= 1/2), the member with the larger strategy ID (Table 1) or, if the two members share the
same strategy, the member whose assessment rule has the larger ID (the sixteen bits d𝑟1𝑟2𝑎1𝑎2
read as a binary number, in the same little-endian convention as in Table 1).
Definition 16 is exhaustive and unambiguous: by Lemma 3, the two members of a mirror pair
have resident reputation equilibria 𝑔∗and 1 −𝑔∗, so either exactly one member satisfies 𝑔∗> 1/2
or both sit at 𝑔∗= 1/2, in which case their strategy IDs differ unless ˆ𝑝= 𝑝, and then their
assessment-rule IDs differ. We call the representatives ‘majority-good’ because, in the generic
case, the representative is the member of the pair whose residents mostly hold good reputations
at the working error rates.
All numerical explorations reported in the main text and in this section evaluate one
representative per mirror class; by Proposition 2, which member is evaluated is immaterial.
Selecting majority-good representatives parallels, at finite error rates, the restriction to self-
approving systems (Corollary 1): a self-approving system satisfies 𝑔∗
𝑝,𝑑> 1/2 for sufficiently
small error rates, and is then the representative of its mirror class.
4.2
Computation of the cooperation and stability indices
We now describe the pipeline used to compute the cooperation and stability indices reported
in the main text (Fig. 2; 𝜒= 𝜖= 0.02) and in the robustness analyses of this section. For each
combination of error rates, we evaluate the 524,800 majority-good representatives of the mirror
classes (Definition 16), obtained by enumerating all 220 moral systems – with conditional or
unconditional action rules – and retaining one representative per mirror class (Proposition 39).
For each representative (𝑝, 𝑑), we first compute the resident reputation equilibrium 𝑔∗
𝑝,𝑑,
locating the unique root in (0, 1) of the quadratic with coefficients (22) by bisection, and from it
the cooperation index 𝜅𝑝,𝑑(23).
The stability indices are estimated by Monte Carlo integration. For each game class
Γ ∈{PD, SG, SH}, we draw once 1,000 payoff configurations uniformly at random from
the normalized payoff region GΓ by rejection sampling: for prisoner’s dilemmas, 𝑇= 1 and 𝑆= 0
are fixed and (𝑅, 𝑃) is drawn uniformly subject to (13); for snowdrift games, 𝑇= 1 and 𝑃= 0
are fixed and (𝑅, 𝑆) is drawn uniformly subject to (14); and for stag hunts, 𝑅= 1 and 𝑆= 0 are
fixed and (𝑇, 𝑃) is drawn uniformly subject to (15). The resulting three samples are archived
and reused for every moral system and every error-rate combination, so that comparisons across
systems and error rates are based on the same payoff configurations. For each system and each
payoff configuration, we then check the stability condition (31) directly: we compute the resident
payoff 𝑤𝑝,𝑑(28) and the mutant payoffs ˜𝑤˜𝑝,𝑝,𝑑(30) for all 15 alternative strategies ˜𝑝≠𝑝, and
record whether 𝑤𝑝,𝑑> ˜𝑤˜𝑝,𝑝,𝑑holds for all of them. The estimate of 𝜎Γ
𝑝,𝑑(𝜖, 𝜒) is the fraction of
the 1,000 payoff configurations at which (𝑝, 𝑑) is stable – a binomial proportion, whose standard
error
√︂
𝜎Γ
𝑝,𝑑

1 −𝜎Γ
𝑝,𝑑

/1,000 is at most
√︁
1/(4 × 1,000) ≈0.016. The average stability index
then follows from Eq. (32).
All computations were implemented in a dedicated Julia package accompanying this paper,
with the archived payoff samples distributed alongside the code; evaluating all representatives
takes on the order of one hour per game class and error-rate combination on a standard desktop
64


computer. The code, the archived payoff samples, and the computed indices are all openly
available; see the Data availability and Code availability statements in the main text.
4.3
Alternative error rates
Fig. 5 shows the stability and cooperation indices of all moral systems for a grid of error-rate
combinations, with execution error rates 𝜖∈{0, 0.01, 0.02, 0.1} and assignment error rates
𝜒∈{0.01, 0.02, 0.05, 0.1}. Larger error rates lower both indices, but the cluster of consistent
discriminating systems in the upper-right corner persists across all combinations examined,
confirming that our results are not an artifact of the error rates chosen for the baseline analysis
(𝜖= 𝜒= 0.02).
Fig. 6 zooms in on the consistent discriminating systems, showing their stability and
cooperation indices over the same grid of error rates so that the variation within the cluster
becomes visible. L2 is among the most stable systems at every combination. At the largest
assignment error rate we examine (𝜒= 0.1), however, the most stable consistent PDISC systems
match or slightly exceed it in stability – the differences lie at the resolution of our Monte Carlo
estimates (Section 4.2) – while cooperating markedly less. Whatever the error rates, the consistent
CDISC systems remain the most cooperative of the consistent discriminating systems.
For completeness, Figs. 7–22 reproduce Fig. 2 of the main text for each of the sixteen
combinations of the error rates 𝜒∈{0.01, 0.02, 0.05, 0.1} and 𝜖∈{0, 0.01, 0.02, 0.1}, with no
jitter applied to the scatter points, so that small differences in stability and cooperation between
individual systems can be read off reliably.

rate 𝜒∈{0.01, 0.02, 0.05, 0.1} (rows), the cooperation index (𝜅) and average stability index (𝜎)
of each of the 524,800 mirror-class representatives (Section 4.1). The consistent discriminating
systems are shown as red circles, the leading eight as green diamonds, and the remaining systems
as a hexbin density layer on a logarithmic color scale.

4.4
Disaggregated stability indices
The average stability index 𝜎(32) used in Fig. 2 of the main text compresses the three classes
of cooperative dilemma into a single number, so that systems with similar averages may owe
their stability to very different classes. Figs. 23–25 therefore plot the stability index of each class
separately. At the small error rates considered (𝜒= 𝜖= 0.02), the Monte Carlo estimates lie
close to the limiting indices derived analytically in Sections 3.6 and 3.7 (Tables 15 and 22), which
they thus corroborate.
The zoomed-in panels b show how the stability of the consistent discriminating systems is
distributed across classes. Except for those using CDISC, all consistent discriminating systems
have one weak class, in which they fall well below the rest and cluster near the limiting index
of 2/3: the systems using ODISC or PDISC in prisoner’s dilemmas (𝜎PD ≈0.6; Fig. 23b), and
the systems using DISC in snowdrift games (𝜎SG ≈0.7; Fig. 24b). No system is weak in stag
hunts, where all consistent discriminating systems are highly stable (𝜎SH > 0.92; Fig. 25b).
Within any single class, the systems using CDISC need not be the most stable: those using DISC
attain comparable values in prisoner’s dilemmas, and those using ODISC or PDISC are stable in
essentially every stag hunt (𝜎SH ≈1). What distinguishes the systems using CDISC is instead
the absence of a weak class, in line with their universal stability (Theorem 1).
The disaggregated indices also clarify the position of the leading eight (green diamonds in
panels a). All eight are highly stable in prisoner’s dilemmas (𝜎PD ≈0.9; Fig. 23a), the class that
contains the donation game for which they were originally derived; outside it, they scatter in line
with Table 22. In snowdrift games (Fig. 24a), only L1 and L2 remain highly stable; L4 and L6 sit
near their limiting index of 2/3, alongside the consistent systems using DISC, while L3, L5, L7,
and L8, which can be invaded by CDISC in every snowdrift game, drop to 𝜎SG ≈0. In stag hunts
(Fig. 25a), L2, L5, L6, and L8 remain highly stable, while L1, L3, L4, and L7, which can be
invaded by unconditional cooperators, fall to 𝜎SH ≈0. L2 is thus the only leading-eight system
that is highly stable in all three classes, and the high average stability of L6 in Fig. 2 of the main
text rests on prisoner’s dilemmas and stag hunts alone.

4.5
Area-weighted stability index
The average stability index 𝜎(32) gives the three classes of cooperative dilemma equal weight.
The area-weighted index 𝜎w of Section 1.10.3 instead weights each class in proportion to the
area of its normalized payoff region. Figs. 26 and 27 show that this choice makes no qualitative
difference. The consistent discriminating systems remain the only cluster to combine high stability
with high cooperation, both at the baseline error rates – where the systems using CDISC are
again the most stable of all – and across the error-rate grid of Fig. 5. The main visible change is a
tighter gap between the cluster and the rest, as the ceiling for systems outside the cluster rises
from 𝜎= 2/3 to 𝜎w = 7/9 (exact values, attained at every error-rate combination we examine);
both are attained by fully uncooperative systems (𝜅= 0), which are stable in every prisoner’s
dilemma and stag hunt but in no snowdrift game.
88


Figure 27: Stability and cooperation indices across a range of error rates, under the area-weighted
index 𝜎w of Section 1.10.3; compare Fig. 5, which uses the equally weighted index 𝜎. Each
panel shows, for one combination of execution error rate 𝜖∈{0, 0.01, 0.02, 0.1} (columns) and
assignment error rate 𝜒∈{0.01, 0.02, 0.05, 0.1} (rows), the cooperation index (𝜅) and area-
weighted stability index (𝜎w) of each of the 524,800 mirror-class representatives (Section 4.1).
The consistent discriminating systems are shown as red circles, the leading eight as green
diamonds, and the remaining systems as a hexbin density layer on a logarithmic color scale.
